# Affordable Care Act

Affordable Care Act

U.S. federal healthcare statute

"Obamacare" redirects here. For the song, see [Obamacare (song)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obamacare_(song) "Obamacare (song)").

Patient Protection and Affordable Care Act

The **Affordable Care Act** ( **ACA**), formally the **Patient Protection and Affordable Care Act** ( **PPACA**) and informally known as **Obamacare**, is a landmark [U.S. federal statute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._federal_statute "U.S. federal statute") enacted by the [111th United States Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/111th_United_States_Congress "111th United States Congress") and signed into law by President [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama "Barack Obama") on March 23, 2010. Together with amendments made to it by the [Health Care and Education Reconciliation Act of 2010](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_Care_and_Education_Reconciliation_Act_of_2010 "Health Care and Education Reconciliation Act of 2010"), it represents the [U.S. healthcare system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Healthcare_in_the_United_States "Healthcare in the United States")'s most significant regulatory overhaul and expansion of coverage since the enactment of [Medicare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicare_(United_States) "Medicare (United States)") and [Medicaid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicaid "Medicaid") in 1965.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Oberlander2010-0)[\[1\]](#refnote-Oberlander2010)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Blumenthal2015-0)[\[2\]](#refnote-Blumenthal2015)[&#8202;](#ref-back-CohenEtAl-0)[\[3\]](#refnote-CohenEtAl)[&#8202;](#ref-back-ReutersSCOTUS-0)[\[4\]](#refnote-ReutersSCOTUS) Most of the act remains in effect.

The ACA's major provisions came into force in 2014. By 2016, the uninsured share of the population had roughly halved, with estimates ranging from 20 to 24 million additional people covered.[&#8202;](#ref-back-CBO_Subsidy2016-0)[\[5\]](#refnote-CBO_Subsidy2016)[&#8202;](#ref-back-HHS_ASPE16-0)[\[6\]](#refnote-HHS_ASPE16) The law also enacted [delivery system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Healthcare_industry#Delivery_of_services "Healthcare industry") reforms intended to constrain healthcare costs and improve quality. After it came into effect, increases in overall healthcare spending slowed, including premiums for employer-based insurance plans.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Kaiser15-0)[\[7\]](#refnote-Kaiser15) The increased coverage was due, roughly equally, to an [expansion of Medicaid eligibility](#Medicaid_expansion) and changes to [individual insurance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Individually_purchased_health_insurance "Individually purchased health insurance") markets. Both received new spending, funded by a combination of new taxes and cuts to Medicare provider rates and [Medicare Advantage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicare_Advantage "Medicare Advantage"). Several [Congressional Budget Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congressional_Budget_Office "Congressional Budget Office") (CBO) reports stated that overall these provisions reduced the [budget deficit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_budget_balance "Government budget balance"), that repealing ACA would increase the deficit,[&#8202;](#ref-back-CBO50252-0)[\[8\]](#refnote-CBO50252)[&#8202;](#ref-back-CBO22077-0)[\[9\]](#refnote-CBO22077) and that the law reduced income inequality.[&#8202;](#ref-back-CBO_Dist14-0)[\[10\]](#refnote-CBO_Dist14)

The act largely retained the existing structure of Medicare, Medicaid, and the [employer market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_insurance_in_the_United_States#Employer_sponsored "Health insurance in the United States"), but individual markets were radically overhauled.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Oberlander2010-1)[\[1\]](#refnote-Oberlander2010)[&#8202;](#ref-back-11-0)[\[11\]](#refnote-11) Insurers were made to [accept all applicants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guaranteed_issue "Guaranteed issue") without charging based on [pre-existing conditions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pre-existing_condition "Pre-existing condition") or demographic status (except age). To combat the resultant [adverse selection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adverse_selection "Adverse selection"), the act [mandated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Individual_shared_responsibility_provision "Individual shared responsibility provision") that individuals buy insurance (or pay a monetary penalty) and that insurers cover a list of " [essential health benefits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Essential_health_benefits "Essential health benefits")". Young people were allowed to stay on their parents' insurance plans until they were 26 years old.

Before and after its enactment the ACA faced strong political opposition, calls for repeal, and [legal challenges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_challenges_to_the_Patient_Protection_and_Affordable_Care_Act "Constitutional challenges to the Patient Protection and Affordable Care Act"). In the _[Sebelius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Federation_of_Independent_Business_v._Sebelius "National Federation of Independent Business v. Sebelius")_ decision, the [U.S. Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_the_United_States "Supreme Court of the United States") ruled that Congress cannot use the [Commerce Clause](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commerce_Clause "Commerce Clause") to compel individuals to buy health insurance but upheld the individual mandate by recharacterizing the penalty as a tax.[&#8202;](#ref-back-12-0)[\[12\]](#refnote-12) The Court also held that states could opt out of the ACA's Medicaid expansion without losing existing federal Medicaid funding, while otherwise upholding the law.[&#8202;](#ref-back-NatLawReview2012-0)[\[13\]](#refnote-NatLawReview2012) This led Republican-controlled states to not participate in Medicaid expansion. Polls initially found that 43% of Americans wanted to see the law repealed or scaled back and 49% wanted to see it expanded or enacted as is.[&#8202;](#ref-back-14-0)[\[14\]](#refnote-14) By 2017, the law had majority support.[&#8202;](#ref-back-15-0)[\[15\]](#refnote-15) The [Tax Cuts and Jobs Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tax_Cuts_and_Jobs_Act "Tax Cuts and Jobs Act") of 2017 set the [individual mandate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Individual_mandate "Individual mandate") penalty at $0 starting in 2019.[&#8202;](#ref-back-16-0)[\[16\]](#refnote-16)[&#8202;](#ref-back-17-0)[\[17\]](#refnote-17)

## Provisions

See also: [Provisions of the Patient Protection and Affordable Care Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Provisions_of_the_Patient_Protection_and_Affordable_Care_Act "Provisions of the Patient Protection and Affordable Care Act")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a1/Barack_Obama_reacts_to_the_passing_of_Healthcare_bill.jpg/500px-Barack_Obama_reacts_to_the_passing_of_Healthcare_bill.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Barack_Obama_reacts_to_the_passing_of_Healthcare_bill.jpg) President Obama and White House staff react to the House of Representatives passing the bill on March 21, 2010.[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/44/View_From_the_Speaker%27s_Office_Tonight_%284452690853%29.jpg/500px-View_From_the_Speaker%27s_Office_Tonight_%284452690853%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:View_From_the_Speaker's_Office_Tonight_(4452690853).jpg) [Jim Clyburn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Clyburn "Jim Clyburn") and Nancy Pelosi celebrate after the House passes the amended bill on March 21.

ACA amended the [Public Health Service Act of 1944](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_Health_Service_Act_of_1944 "Public Health Service Act of 1944") and inserted new provisions on affordable care into [Title 42 of the United States Code](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Title_42_of_the_United_States_Code "Title 42 of the United States Code").[&#8202;](#ref-back-Oberlander2010-2)[\[1\]](#refnote-Oberlander2010)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Blumenthal2015-1)[\[2\]](#refnote-Blumenthal2015)[&#8202;](#ref-back-CohenEtAl-1)[\[3\]](#refnote-CohenEtAl)[&#8202;](#ref-back-24health-0)[\[18\]](#refnote-24health)[&#8202;](#ref-back-ReutersSCOTUS-1)[\[4\]](#refnote-ReutersSCOTUS) The individual insurance market was radically overhauled, and many of the law's regulations applied specifically to this market,[&#8202;](#ref-back-Oberlander2010-3)[\[1\]](#refnote-Oberlander2010) while the structure of Medicare, Medicaid, and the [employer market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_insurance_in_the_United_States#Employer_sponsored "Health insurance in the United States") were largely retained.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Blumenthal2015-2)[\[2\]](#refnote-Blumenthal2015) Some regulations applied to the employer market, and the law also made delivery system changes that affected most of the health care system.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Blumenthal2015-3)[\[2\]](#refnote-Blumenthal2015)

Further information: [Commission on Key National Indicators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commission_on_Key_National_Indicators "Commission on Key National Indicators")

### Insurance regulations: individual policies

All new individual major medical health insurance policies sold to individuals and families faced new requirements.[&#8202;](#ref-back-19-0)[\[19\]](#refnote-19) The requirements took effect on January 1, 2014. They include:

- [Guaranteed issue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guaranteed_issue "Guaranteed issue") prohibits insurers from denying coverage to individuals because of [preexisting conditions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Preexisting_condition "Preexisting condition").[&#8202;](#ref-back-20-0)[\[20\]](#refnote-20)
- States were required to ensure the availability of insurance for individual children who did not have coverage via their families.
- A partial [community rating](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Community_rating "Community rating") allows premiums to vary only by age and location, regardless of preexisting conditions. Premiums for older applicants can be no more than three times those for the youngest.[&#8202;](#ref-back-21-0)[\[21\]](#refnote-21)
- [Essential health benefits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Essential_health_benefits "Essential health benefits") must be provided. The [National Academy of Medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Academy_of_Medicine "National Academy of Medicine") defines the law's "essential health benefits" as "ambulatory patient services; emergency services; hospitalization; maternity and newborn care; mental health and substance use disorder services, including behavioral health treatment; prescription drugs; rehabilitative and habilitative services and devices; laboratory services; preventive and wellness services and chronic disease management; and pediatric services, including oral and vision care"[&#8202;](#ref-back-Hhs.gov-0)[\[22\]](#refnote-Hhs.gov)[&#8202;](#ref-back-essential-0)[\[23\]](#refnote-essential) and others[&#8202;](#ref-back-24-0)[\[24\]](#refnote-24) rated Level A or B[&#8202;](#ref-back-25-0)[\[25\]](#refnote-25) by the [U.S. Preventive Services Task Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Preventive_Services_Task_Force "United States Preventive Services Task Force").[&#8202;](#ref-back-ReferenceB-0)[\[26\]](#refnote-ReferenceB) In determining essential benefits, the law required that standard benefits should offer at least that of a "typical employer plan".[&#8202;](#ref-back-KaiserEHB-0)[\[27\]](#refnote-KaiserEHB) States may require additional services.[&#8202;](#ref-back-28-0)[\[28\]](#refnote-28)
- Preventive care and screenings for women.[&#8202;](#ref-back-29-0)[\[29\]](#refnote-29) "\[A\]ll Food and Drug Administration approved [contraceptive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contraception "Contraception") methods, sterilization procedures, and patient education and counseling for all women with reproductive capacity".[&#8202;](#ref-back-30-0)[\[30\]](#refnote-30) This mandate applies to all employers and educational institutions except for religious organizations.[&#8202;](#ref-back-faqs-0)[\[31\]](#refnote-faqs)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Kliff-0)[\[32\]](#refnote-Kliff) These regulations were included on the recommendations of the [Institute of Medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institute_of_Medicine "Institute of Medicine").[&#8202;](#ref-back-FinalRule-0)[\[33\]](#refnote-FinalRule)[&#8202;](#ref-back-cnncontra-0)[\[34\]](#refnote-cnncontra)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bf/Health_Care_Delivery_System_Reform_and_The_Patient_Protection_%26_Affordable_Care_Act.pdf/page1-500px-Health_Care_Delivery_System_Reform_and_The_Patient_Protection_%26_Affordable_Care_Act.pdf.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Health_Care_Delivery_System_Reform_and_The_Patient_Protection_&_Affordable_Care_Act.pdf) In 2012 Senator [Sheldon Whitehouse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sheldon_Whitehouse "Sheldon Whitehouse") created this summary to explain his view on the act.

- Annual and lifetime coverage caps on essential benefits were banned.[&#8202;](#ref-back-healthcare-0)[\[35\]](#refnote-healthcare)[&#8202;](#ref-back-hhs-0)[\[36\]](#refnote-hhs)[&#8202;](#ref-back-37-0)[\[37\]](#refnote-37)
- Insurers are forbidden from dropping [policyholders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Policyholder "Policyholder") when they become ill.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Top_18-0)[\[38\]](#refnote-Top_18)[&#8202;](#ref-back-39-0)[\[39\]](#refnote-39)
- All policies must provide an annual maximum out-of-pocket (MOOP) payment cap for an individual's or family's medical expenses (excluding premiums). After the MOOP payment is reached, all remaining costs must be paid by the insurer.[&#8202;](#ref-back-40-0)[\[40\]](#refnote-40)
- Preventive care, vaccinations and medical screenings cannot be subject to [co-payments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copayment "Copayment"), [co-insurance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Co-insurance#In_health_insurance "Co-insurance") or [deductibles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deductible#Health_and_travel_insurance "Deductible").[&#8202;](#ref-back-SHNS-0)[\[41\]](#refnote-SHNS)[&#8202;](#ref-back-KaiserSummary-0)[\[42\]](#refnote-KaiserSummary)[&#8202;](#ref-back-cmsprev-0)[\[43\]](#refnote-cmsprev) Specific examples of covered services include: [mammograms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mammograms "Mammograms") and [colonoscopies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colonoscopy "Colonoscopy"), wellness visits, [gestational diabetes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gestational_diabetes "Gestational diabetes") screening, [HPV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_papillomavirus "Human papillomavirus") testing, [STI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sexually_transmitted_infection "Sexually transmitted infection") counseling, [HIV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_immunodeficiency_virus "Human immunodeficiency virus") screening and counseling, contraceptive methods, breastfeeding support/supplies and [domestic violence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domestic_violence "Domestic violence") screening and counseling.[&#8202;](#ref-back-SchiffHardinLLP-0)[\[44\]](#refnote-SchiffHardinLLP)
- The law established four tiers of coverage: bronze, silver, gold and platinum. All categories offer essential health benefits. The categories vary in their division of premiums and out-of-pocket costs: bronze plans have the lowest monthly premiums and highest out-of-pocket costs, while platinum plans are the reverse.[&#8202;](#ref-back-KaiserEHB-1)[\[27\]](#refnote-KaiserEHB)[&#8202;](#ref-back-choose-0)[\[45\]](#refnote-choose) The percentages of [health care costs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_care_prices_in_the_United_States "Health care prices in the United States") that plans are expected to cover through premiums (as opposed to out-of-pocket costs) are, on average: 60% (bronze), 70% (silver), 80% (gold), and 90% (platinum).[&#8202;](#ref-back-46-0)[\[46\]](#refnote-46)
- Insurers are required to implement an [appeals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appeal "Appeal") process for coverage determination and claims on all new plans.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Top_18-1)[\[38\]](#refnote-Top_18)
- Insurers must [spend at least 80–85% of premium dollars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loss_ratio#Medical_loss_ratio "Loss ratio") on health costs; rebates must be issued if this is violated.[&#8202;](#ref-back-cmsprog-0)[\[47\]](#refnote-cmsprog)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FederalRegister-0)[\[48\]](#refnote-FederalRegister)

### Individual mandate

The [individual mandate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_insurance_mandate#Affordable_Care_Act "Health insurance mandate")[&#8202;](#ref-back-AHPAMandate-0)[\[49\]](#refnote-AHPAMandate) required everyone to have insurance or [pay a penalty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Provisions_of_the_Patient_Protection_and_Affordable_Care_Act#Effective_January_1,_2014 "Provisions of the Patient Protection and Affordable Care Act"). The mandate and limits on open enrollment[&#8202;](#ref-back-CohnOE-0)[\[50\]](#refnote-CohnOE)[&#8202;](#ref-back-LimitedOEForbes-0)[\[51\]](#refnote-LimitedOEForbes) were designed to avoid the [insurance death spiral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_spiral_(insurance) "Death spiral (insurance)"), minimize the [free rider problem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_rider_problem "Free rider problem") and prevent the healthcare system from succumbing to [adverse selection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adverse_selection "Adverse selection").

The mandate was intended to increase the size and diversity of the insured population, including more young and healthy participants to broaden the [risk pool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Risk_pool "Risk pool"), spreading costs.[&#8202;](#ref-back-52-0)[\[52\]](#refnote-52)

Among the groups who were not subject to the individual mandate are:

- [Undocumented immigrants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Illegal_immigrant_population_of_the_United_States "Illegal immigrant population of the United States"). Estimated at 8million, roughly a third of the 23 million projection, they are ineligible for insurance [subsidies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subsidies "Subsidies") and Medicaid.[&#8202;](#ref-back-cbouninsured-0)[\[53\]](#refnote-cbouninsured)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Chaikind2011-0)[\[54\]](#refnote-Chaikind2011) They remain eligible for emergency services.
- Medicaid-eligible citizens not enrolled in Medicaid.[&#8202;](#ref-back-notcovered-0)[\[55\]](#refnote-notcovered)
- Citizens whose insurance coverage would cost more than 8% of household income.[&#8202;](#ref-back-notcovered-1)[\[55\]](#refnote-notcovered)
- Citizens who live in [states that opt-out of Medicaid expansion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicaid_coverage_gap "Medicaid coverage gap") and who qualify for neither existing Medicaid coverage nor subsidized coverage.[&#8202;](#ref-back-optout-0)[\[56\]](#refnote-optout)

The [Tax Cuts and Jobs Act of 2017](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tax_Cuts_and_Jobs_Act_of_2017 "Tax Cuts and Jobs Act of 2017"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-hatchsays-0)[\[57\]](#refnote-hatchsays) set to $0 the penalty for not complying with the individual mandate, starting in 2019.[&#8202;](#ref-back-auto-0)[\[58\]](#refnote-auto)

### Exchanges

Main article: [Health insurance marketplace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_insurance_marketplace "Health insurance marketplace")

ACA mandated that health insurance exchanges be provided for each state. The exchanges are regulated, largely online marketplaces, administered by either federal or state governments, where individuals, families and small businesses can purchase private insurance plans.[&#8202;](#ref-back-HealthCareGov-0)[\[59\]](#refnote-HealthCareGov)[&#8202;](#ref-back-aphabasics-0)[\[60\]](#refnote-aphabasics)[&#8202;](#ref-back-CohnExchanges-0)[\[61\]](#refnote-CohnExchanges) Exchanges first offered insurance for 2014. Some exchanges also provide access to Medicaid.[&#8202;](#ref-back-62-0)[\[62\]](#refnote-62)[&#8202;](#ref-back-63-0)[\[63\]](#refnote-63)

States that set up their own exchanges have some discretion on standards and prices.[&#8202;](#ref-back-KaiserExchangesNotes-0)[\[64\]](#refnote-KaiserExchangesNotes)[&#8202;](#ref-back-cwfstate-0)[\[65\]](#refnote-cwfstate) For example, states approve plans for sale, and thereby influence (through negotiations) prices. They can impose additional coverage requirements—such as abortion.[&#8202;](#ref-back-66-0)[\[66\]](#refnote-66) Alternatively, states can make the federal government responsible for operating their exchanges.[&#8202;](#ref-back-KaiserExchangesNotes-1)[\[64\]](#refnote-KaiserExchangesNotes)

### Premium subsidies

Individuals whose household incomes are between 100% and 400% of the [federal poverty level](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_poverty_level "Federal poverty level") (FPL) are eligible to receive [federal subsidies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subsidy "Subsidy") for premiums for policies purchased on an ACA exchange, provided they are not eligible for [Medicare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicare_(United_States) "Medicare (United States)"), [Medicaid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicaid "Medicaid"), the [Children's Health Insurance Program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Children's_Health_Insurance_Program "Children's Health Insurance Program"), or other forms of public assistance health coverage, and do not have access to affordable coverage (no more than 9.86% of income for the employee's coverage) through their own or a family member's employer.[&#8202;](#ref-back-KaiserFamily-0)[\[67\]](#refnote-KaiserFamily)[&#8202;](#ref-back-TamiLuhby-0)[\[68\]](#refnote-TamiLuhby)[&#8202;](#ref-back-hip-dhhs-0)[\[69\]](#refnote-hip-dhhs) Households below the federal poverty level are not eligible to receive these subsidies. Lawful Residents and some other legally present immigrants whose household income is below 100% FPL and are not otherwise eligible for Medicaid are eligible for subsidies if they meet all other eligibility requirements.[&#8202;](#ref-back-70-0)[\[70\]](#refnote-70)[&#8202;](#ref-back-KaiserFamily-1)[\[67\]](#refnote-KaiserFamily) Married people must file taxes jointly to receive subsidies. Enrollees must have U.S. citizenship or proof of legal residency to obtain a subsidy.

The subsidies for an ACA plan purchased on an exchange stop at 400% of the federal poverty level (FPL). According to the Kaiser Foundation, this results in a sharp "discontinuity of treatment" at 400% FPL, which is sometimes called the "subsidy cliff".[&#8202;](#ref-back-71-0)[\[71\]](#refnote-71) After-subsidy premiums for the second lowest cost silver plan (SCLSP) just below the cliff are 9.86% of income in 2019.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:82-0)[\[72\]](#refnote-:82)

Subsidies are provided as an advanceable, [refundable tax credit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tax_credit#State_tax_credits "Tax credit").[&#8202;](#ref-back-sec1401-0)[\[73\]](#refnote-sec1401)[&#8202;](#ref-back-sec1401_p-0)[\[74\]](#refnote-sec1401_p)

The amount of subsidy is sufficient to reduce the premium for the second-lowest-cost silver plan (SCLSP) on an exchange to a sliding-scale percentage of income. The percentage is based on the percent of federal poverty level (FPL) for the household, and varies slightly from year to year. In 2019, it ranged from 2.08% of income (100%-133% FPL) to 9.86% of income (300%-400% FPL).[&#8202;](#ref-back-hip-dhhs-1)[\[69\]](#refnote-hip-dhhs) The subsidy can be used for any plan available on the exchange, but not catastrophic plans. The subsidy may not exceed the premium for the purchased plan.

(In this section, the term "income" refers to modified [adjusted gross income](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adjusted_gross_income "Adjusted gross income").[&#8202;](#ref-back-KaiserFamily-2)[\[67\]](#refnote-KaiserFamily)[&#8202;](#ref-back-75-0)[\[75\]](#refnote-75))

Small businesses are eligible for a tax credit provided they enroll in the [SHOP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Small_Business_Health_Options_Program "Small Business Health Options Program") Marketplace.[&#8202;](#ref-back-IRSBusinessSubsidy-0)[\[76\]](#refnote-IRSBusinessSubsidy)

| Income% of [federal poverty level](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_poverty_level "Federal poverty level") | Premium cap as a share of income | Income[a](#endnote_fedpovlevel) | Maximum[b](#endnote_max) annual net premium after subsidy<br>(second-lowest-cost silver plan) | Maximum out-of-pocket |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 133% | 3.11% of income | $33,383 | $1,038 | $5,200 |
| 150% | 4.15% of income | $37,650 | $1,562 | $5,200 |
| 200% | 6.54% of income | $50,200 | $3,283 | $5,200 |
| 250% | 8.36% of income | $62,750 | $5,246 | $12,600 |
| 300% | 9.86% of income | $75,300 | $7,425 | $15,800 |
| 400% | 9.86% of income | $100,400 | $9,899 | $15,800 |
| a.**[^](#ref_fedpovlevel)**In 2019, the federal poverty level was $25,100 for family of four (outside of Alaska and Hawaii).<br>b.**[^](#ref_max)**If the premium for the second lowest cost silver plan (SLCSP) is greater than the amount in this column, the amount of the premium subsidy will be such that it brings the net cost of the SCLSP down to the amount in this column. Otherwise, there will be no subsidy, and the SLCSP premium will (of course) be no more than (usually less than) the amount in this column.<br>Note: The numbers in the table do not apply for Alaska and Hawaii. |  |  |  |  |

Maximum net premium after subsidies (2019) for family of four[&#8202;](#ref-back-:82-1)[\[72\]](#refnote-:82)

### Cost-sharing reduction subsidies

Main article: [Cost sharing reductions subsidy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cost_sharing_reductions_subsidy "Cost sharing reductions subsidy")

As written, ACA mandated that insurers reduce copayments and deductibles for ACA exchange enrollees earning less than 250% of the FPL. Medicaid recipients were not eligible for the reductions.

So-called cost-sharing reduction (CSR) subsidies were to be paid to insurance companies to fund the reductions. During 2017, approximately $7 billion in CSR subsidies were to be paid, versus $34 billion for premium tax credits.[&#8202;](#ref-back-77-0)[\[77\]](#refnote-77)

The latter was defined as [mandatory spending](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mandatory_spending "Mandatory spending") that does not require an annual Congressional appropriation. CSR payments were not explicitly defined as mandatory. This led to litigation and disruption later.\[ _[further explanation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Please_clarify "Wikipedia:Please clarify")_\]

### Risk management

#### Risk corridors

The [risk-corridor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Risk_corridor "Risk corridor") program was a temporary risk management device.[&#8202;](#ref-back-78-0)[\[78\]](#refnote-78):1 It was intended to encourage reluctant insurers into ACA insurance market from 2014 to 2016. For those years the [Department of Health and Human Services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Department_of_Health_and_Human_Services "Department of Health and Human Services") (DHHS) would cover some of the losses for insurers whose plans performed worse than they expected. Loss-making insurers would receive payments paid for in part by profit-making insurers.[&#8202;](#ref-back-79-0)[\[79\]](#refnote-79)[&#8202;](#ref-back-80-0)[\[80\]](#refnote-80)\[ _[attribution needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Attribution_needed "Wikipedia:Attribution needed")_\] Similar risk corridors had been established for the [Medicare prescription drug benefit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicare_Part_D "Medicare Part D").[&#8202;](#ref-back-81-0)[\[81\]](#refnote-81)

While many insurers initially offered exchange plans, the program did not pay for itself as planned, losing up to $8.3 billion for 2014 and 2015. Authorization had to be given so DHHS could pay insurers from "general government revenues".\[ _[attribution needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Attribution_needed "Wikipedia:Attribution needed")_\] However, the [Consolidated Appropriations Act, 2014](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consolidated_Appropriations_Act,_2014 "Consolidated Appropriations Act, 2014") stated that no funds "could be used for risk-corridor payments".[&#8202;](#ref-back-82-0)[\[82\]](#refnote-82)\[ _[attribution needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Attribution_needed "Wikipedia:Attribution needed")_\] leaving the government in a potential breach of contract with insurers who offered qualified health plans.[&#8202;](#ref-back-latimes_GOP_ACA_corridor-0)[\[83\]](#refnote-latimes_GOP_ACA_corridor)

Several insurers sued the government at the [United States Court of Federal Claims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Court_of_Federal_Claims "United States Court of Federal Claims") to recover the funds believed owed to them under the Risk Corridors program. While several were summarily closed, in the case of _[Moda Health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moda_Health "Moda Health") v the United States_, Moda Health won a $214-million judgment in February 2017. Federal Claims judge [Thomas C. Wheeler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thomas_C._Wheeler "Thomas C. Wheeler") stated, "the Government made a promise in the risk corridors program that it has yet to fulfill. Today, the court directs the Government to fulfill that promise."[&#8202;](#ref-back-84-0)[\[84\]](#refnote-84) Moda Health's case was appealed by the government to the [United States Court of Appeals for the Federal Circuit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Court_of_Appeals_for_the_Federal_Circuit "United States Court of Appeals for the Federal Circuit") along with the appeals of the other insurers; here, the Federal Circuit reversed the Moda Health ruling and ruled across all the cases in favor of the government, that the appropriations riders ceded the government from paying out remain money due to the insurers. The Supreme Court reversed this ruling in the consolidated case, _[Maine Community Health Options v. United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maine_Community_Health_Options_v._United_States "Maine Community Health Options v. United States")_, reaffirming as with Judge Wheeler that the government had a responsibility to pay those funds under the ACA and the use of riders to de-obligate its from those payments was illegal.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:1-0)[\[85\]](#refnote-:1)

#### Reinsurance

The temporary reinsurance program is meant to stabilize premiums by reducing the incentive for insurers to raise premiums due to concerns about higher-risk enrollees. Reinsurance was based on retrospective costs rather than prospective risk evaluations. Reinsurance was available from 2014 through 2016.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:4-0)[\[86\]](#refnote-:4)

#### Risk adjustment

Risk adjustment involves transferring funds from plans with lower-risk enrollees to plans with higher-risk enrollees. It was intended to encourage insurers to compete based on value and efficiency rather than by attracting healthier enrollees. Of the three risk management programs, only risk adjustment was permanent. Plans with low actuarial risk compensate plans with high actuarial risk.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:4-1)[\[86\]](#refnote-:4)

### Medicaid expansion

Main article: [Medicaid coverage gap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicaid_coverage_gap "Medicaid coverage gap")

ACA revised and expanded [Medicaid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicaid "Medicaid") eligibility starting in 2014. All U.S. citizens and legal residents with income up to 133% of the [poverty line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poverty_line_in_the_United_States#Measures_of_poverty "Poverty line in the United States") would qualify for coverage in any state that participated in the Medicaid program. Previously, states could set various lower thresholds for certain groups and were not required to cover adults without dependent children. The federal government was to pay 100% of the increased cost in 2014, 2015 and 2016; 95% in 2017, 94% in 2018, 93% in 2019, and 90% in 2020 and all subsequent years.[&#8202;](#ref-back-87-0)[\[87\]](#refnote-87)[&#8202;](#ref-back-88-0)[\[88\]](#refnote-88)[&#8202;](#ref-back-89-0)[\[89\]](#refnote-89)[&#8202;](#ref-back-90-0)[\[90\]](#refnote-90) A 5% "income disregard" made the effective income eligibility limit for Medicaid 138% of the poverty level.[&#8202;](#ref-back-138fpl-0)[\[91\]](#refnote-138fpl) However, the [Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_the_United_States "Supreme Court of the United States") ruled in _[NFIB v. Sebelius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Federation_of_Independent_Business_v._Sebelius "National Federation of Independent Business v. Sebelius")_ that this provision of ACA was coercive, and that states could choose to continue at pre-ACA eligibility levels.

### Medicare savings

Medicare reimbursements were reduced to insurers and drug companies for private [Medicare Advantage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicare_Advantage "Medicare Advantage") policies that the [Government Accountability Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_Accountability_Office "Government Accountability Office") and [Medicare Payment Advisory Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicare_Payment_Advisory_Commission "Medicare Payment Advisory Commission") found to be excessively costly relative to standard Medicare;[&#8202;](#ref-back-CMSMedicarePPACA-0)[\[92\]](#refnote-CMSMedicarePPACA)[&#8202;](#ref-back-93-0)[\[93\]](#refnote-93) and to hospitals that failed standards of efficiency and care.[&#8202;](#ref-back-CMSMedicarePPACA-1)[\[92\]](#refnote-CMSMedicarePPACA)

### Taxes

#### Medicare taxes

Income from self-employment and wages of single individuals in excess of $200,000 annually are subjected to an additional tax of 0.9%. The threshold amount is $250,000 for a married couple filing jointly (threshold applies to their total compensation), or $125,000 for a married person filing separately.[&#8202;](#ref-back-94-0)[\[94\]](#refnote-94)

In ACA's companion legislation, the [Health Care and Education Reconciliation Act of 2010](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_Care_and_Education_Reconciliation_Act_of_2010 "Health Care and Education Reconciliation Act of 2010"), an additional tax of 3.8% was applied to unearned income, specifically the lesser of net investment income and the amount by which adjusted gross income exceeds the above income limits.[&#8202;](#ref-back-95-0)[\[95\]](#refnote-95)

#### Excise taxes

ACA included an excise tax of 40% (" [Cadillac tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cadillac_tax "Cadillac tax")") on total employer premium spending in excess of specified dollar amounts (initially $10,200 for single coverage and $27,500 for family coverage[&#8202;](#ref-back-96-0)[\[96\]](#refnote-96)) indexed to inflation. This tax was originally scheduled to take effect in 2018, but was delayed until 2020 by the [Consolidated Appropriations Act, 2016](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consolidated_Appropriations_Act,_2016 "Consolidated Appropriations Act, 2016") and again to 2022. The excise tax on high-cost health plans was completely repealed as part of H.R.1865 - Further Consolidated Appropriations Act, 2020.

Excise taxes totaling $3 billion were levied on importers and manufacturers of prescription drugs. An excise tax of 2.3% on medical devices and a 10% excise tax on indoor tanning services were applied as well.[&#8202;](#ref-back-97-0)[\[97\]](#refnote-97) The tax was repealed in late 2019.[&#8202;](#ref-back-auto1-0)[\[98\]](#refnote-auto1)

### SCHIP

The [State Children's Health Insurance Program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_Children's_Health_Insurance_Program "State Children's Health Insurance Program") (CHIP) enrollment process was simplified.[&#8202;](#ref-back-99-0)[\[99\]](#refnote-99)[&#8202;](#ref-back-KFF1-0)[\[100\]](#refnote-KFF1)

### Dependents

Beginning September 23, 2010, dependents were permitted to remain on their parents' insurance plan until their 26th birthday, including dependents who no longer lived with their parents, are not a dependent on a parent's tax return, are no longer a student, or are married.[&#8202;](#ref-back-H.R.3590Enrolled-0)[\[101\]](#refnote-H.R.3590Enrolled)[&#8202;](#ref-back-102-0)[\[102\]](#refnote-102)

### Employer mandate

Businesses that employ fifty or more people but do not offer health insurance to their full-time employees are assessed additional tax if the government has subsidized a full-time employee's healthcare through tax deductions or other means. This is commonly known as the [employer mandate](#Employer_mandate_and_part-time_working_hours).[&#8202;](#ref-back-kffpay-0)[\[103\]](#refnote-kffpay)[&#8202;](#ref-back-WSJ-mar25-0)[\[104\]](#refnote-WSJ-mar25) This provision was included to encourage employers to continue providing insurance once the exchanges began operating.[&#8202;](#ref-back-http-0)[\[105\]](#refnote-http)

### Delivery system reforms

The act includes [delivery system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Healthcare_industry#Delivery_of_services "Healthcare industry") reforms intended to constrain costs and improve quality. These include Medicare payment changes to discourage [hospital-acquired conditions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hospital-acquired_condition "Hospital-acquired condition") and [readmissions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hospital_readmission "Hospital readmission"), [bundled payment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bundled_payment "Bundled payment") initiatives, the [Center for Medicare and Medicaid Innovation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Center_for_Medicare_and_Medicaid_Innovation "Center for Medicare and Medicaid Innovation"), the [Independent Payment Advisory Board](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independent_Payment_Advisory_Board "Independent Payment Advisory Board"), and [accountable care organizations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Accountable_care_organization "Accountable care organization").

#### Hospital quality

Health care cost/quality initiatives included incentives to [reduce hospital infections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hospital-acquired_infection "Hospital-acquired infection"), adopt [electronic medical records](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electronic_medical_record "Electronic medical record"), and to coordinate care and prioritize quality over quantity.[&#8202;](#ref-back-NYMagChait2-0)[\[106\]](#refnote-NYMagChait2)

#### Bundled payments

[Medicare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicare_(United_States) "Medicare (United States)") switched from [fee-for-service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fee-for-service "Fee-for-service") to [bundled payments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bundled_payment "Bundled payment").[&#8202;](#ref-back-107-0)[\[107\]](#refnote-107)[&#8202;](#ref-back-108-0)[\[108\]](#refnote-108) A single payment was to be paid to a hospital and a physician group for a defined episode of care (such as a [hip replacement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hip_replacement "Hip replacement")) rather than separate payments to individual service providers.[&#8202;](#ref-back-109-0)[\[109\]](#refnote-109)

#### Accountable care organizations

Main article: [Accountable care organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Accountable_care_organization "Accountable care organization")

The [Medicare Shared Savings Program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicare_Shared_Savings_Program "Medicare Shared Savings Program") (MSSP) was established by section 3022 of the Affordable Care Act. It is the program by which an [accountable care organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Accountable_care_organization "Accountable care organization") interacts with the federal government, and by which accountable care organizations can be created.[&#8202;](#ref-back-110-0)[\[110\]](#refnote-110) It is a [fee-for-service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fee-for-service "Fee-for-service") model.

The Act allowed the creation of accountable care organizations (ACOs), which are groups of doctors, hospitals and other providers that commit to give coordinated care to Medicare patients. ACOs were allowed to continue using [fee-for-service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fee-for-service "Fee-for-service") billing. They receive bonus payments from the government for minimizing costs while achieving quality benchmarks that emphasize prevention and mitigation of [chronic disease](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chronic_disease "Chronic disease"). Missing cost or quality benchmarks subjected them to penalties.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:15-0)[\[111\]](#refnote-:15)

Unlike [health maintenance organizations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_maintenance_organization "Health maintenance organization"), ACO patients are not required to obtain all care from the ACO. Also, unlike HMOs, ACOs must achieve quality-of-care goals.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:15-1)[\[111\]](#refnote-:15)

### Medicare drug benefit (Part D)

Medicare Part D participants received a 50% discount on brand name drugs purchased after exhausting their [initial coverage and before reaching the catastrophic-coverage threshold](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicare_Part_D_coverage_gap "Medicare Part D coverage gap").[&#8202;](#ref-back-112-0)[\[112\]](#refnote-112) By 2020, the "doughnut hole" would be completely filled.[&#8202;](#ref-back-113-0)[\[113\]](#refnote-113)

### State waivers

From 2017 onwards, states can apply for a "waiver for state innovation" which allows them to conduct experiments that meet certain criteria.[&#8202;](#ref-back-GPOStateWaiver-0)[\[114\]](#refnote-GPOStateWaiver) To obtain a waiver, a state must pass legislation setting up an alternative health system that provides insurance at least as comprehensive and as affordable as ACA, covers at least as many residents and does not increase the federal deficit.[&#8202;](#ref-back-StateWaiverConditions-0)[\[115\]](#refnote-StateWaiverConditions) These states can escape some of ACA's central requirements, including the individual and employer mandates and the provision of an insurance exchange.[&#8202;](#ref-back-WaPoWaiverFlexibility-0)[\[116\]](#refnote-WaPoWaiverFlexibility) The state would receive compensation equal to the aggregate amount of any federal subsidies and tax credits for which its residents and employers would have been eligible under ACA, if they cannot be paid under the state plan.[&#8202;](#ref-back-GPOStateWaiver-1)[\[114\]](#refnote-GPOStateWaiver)

### Other insurance provisions

The [Community Living Assistance Services and Supports Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Community_Living_Assistance_Services_and_Supports_Act "Community Living Assistance Services and Supports Act") (or CLASS Act) established a voluntary and public [long-term care insurance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Long-term_care_insurance "Long-term care insurance") option for employees,[&#8202;](#ref-back-117-0)[\[117\]](#refnote-117)[&#8202;](#ref-back-multiple2-0)[\[118\]](#refnote-multiple2)[&#8202;](#ref-back-119-0)[\[119\]](#refnote-119) The program was abolished as impractical without ever having taken effect.[&#8202;](#ref-back-120-0)[\[120\]](#refnote-120)

Consumer Operated and Oriented Plans (CO-OP), member-governed non-profit insurers, could start providing health care coverage, based on a 5-year federal loan.[&#8202;](#ref-back-121-0)[\[121\]](#refnote-121) As of 2017, only four of the original 23 co-ops were still in operation,[&#8202;](#ref-back-122-0)[\[122\]](#refnote-122) including [Mountain Health CO-OP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mountain_Health_CO-OP "Mountain Health CO-OP").

### Nutrition labeling requirements

[Nutrition labeling requirements](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nutrition_labeling_requirements_of_the_Affordable_Care_Act "Nutrition labeling requirements of the Affordable Care Act") officially took effect in 2010, but implementation was delayed, and they actually took effect on May 7, 2018.[&#8202;](#ref-back-123-0)[\[123\]](#refnote-123)

## Legislative history

Main articles: [Health care reform in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_care_reform_in_the_United_States "Health care reform in the United States") and [Health care reform debate in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_care_reform_debate_in_the_United_States "Health care reform debate in the United States")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f5/Obama_signs_health_care-20100323.jpg/960px-Obama_signs_health_care-20100323.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Obama_signs_health_care-20100323.jpg) President Obama signs the Patient Protection and Affordable Care Act on March 23, 2010.

ACA followed a long series of unsuccessful attempts by one party or the other to pass major insurance reforms. Innovations were limited to [health savings accounts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_savings_account "Health savings account") (2003), [medical savings accounts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medical_savings_account_(United_States) "Medical savings account (United States)") (1996) or [flexible spending accounts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flexible_spending_account "Flexible spending account"), which increased insurance options, but did not materially expand coverage. Health care was a major factor in multiple elections, but until 2009, neither party had the votes to overcome the other's opposition.

### Individual mandate

The concept of an individual mandate goes back to at least 1989, when [The Heritage Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Heritage_Foundation "The Heritage Foundation"), a [conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservatism_in_the_United_States "Conservatism in the United States") think-tank, proposed an individual mandate as an alternative to [single-payer health care](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Single-payer_health_care "Single-payer health care").[&#8202;](#ref-back-forbes1-0)[\[124\]](#refnote-forbes1)[&#8202;](#ref-back-125-0)[\[125\]](#refnote-125) It was championed for a time by conservative economists and [Republican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_Party_(United_States) "Republican Party (United States)") senators as a market-based approach to healthcare reform on the basis of individual responsibility and avoidance of [free rider problems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_rider_problem "Free rider problem"). Specifically, because the 1986 [Emergency Medical Treatment and Active Labor Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emergency_Medical_Treatment_and_Active_Labor_Act "Emergency Medical Treatment and Active Labor Act") (EMTALA) requires any hospital participating in Medicare (nearly all do) to provide emergency care to anyone who needs it, the government often indirectly bore the cost of those without the ability to pay.[&#8202;](#ref-back-CommonSense-0)[\[126\]](#refnote-CommonSense)[&#8202;](#ref-back-nyt-mandate-0)[\[127\]](#refnote-nyt-mandate)[&#8202;](#ref-back-new-yorker-klein-0)[\[128\]](#refnote-new-yorker-klein)

President [Bill Clinton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_Clinton "Bill Clinton") [proposed a major healthcare reform bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clinton_health_care_plan_of_1993 "Clinton health care plan of 1993") in 1993[&#8202;](#ref-back-nyt-mandate-1)[\[127\]](#refnote-nyt-mandate) that ultimately failed.[&#8202;](#ref-back-129-0)[\[129\]](#refnote-129) Clinton negotiated a compromise with the [105th Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/105th_United_States_Congress "105th United States Congress") to instead enact the [State Children's Health Insurance Program (SCHIP)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_Children's_Health_Insurance_Program "State Children's Health Insurance Program") in 1997.[&#8202;](#ref-back-130-0)[\[130\]](#refnote-130) The failed Clinton plan included a mandate for employers to provide health insurance to all employees through a regulated marketplace of [health maintenance organizations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_maintenance_organization "Health maintenance organization"). Republican senators proposed an alternative that would have required individuals, but not employers, to buy insurance.

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/3/37/John_Chafee.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail_unscaled)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:John_Chafee.jpg) John Chafee

The 1993 Republican [Health Equity and Access Reform Today (HEART) Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_Equity_and_Access_Reform_Today_Act "Health Equity and Access Reform Today Act"), contained a "universal coverage" requirement with a penalty for noncompliance—an individual mandate—as well as subsidies to be used in state-based 'purchasing groups'.[&#8202;](#ref-back-kaiserhealthnews1993-0)[\[131\]](#refnote-kaiserhealthnews1993) Advocates included prominent Republican senators such as [John Chafee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Chafee "John Chafee"), [Orrin Hatch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orrin_Hatch "Orrin Hatch"), [Chuck Grassley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chuck_Grassley "Chuck Grassley"), [Bob Bennett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bob_Bennett_(politician) "Bob Bennett (politician)") and [Kit Bond](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kit_Bond "Kit Bond").[&#8202;](#ref-back-132-0)[\[132\]](#refnote-132)[&#8202;](#ref-back-133-0)[\[133\]](#refnote-133) The 1994 Republican Consumer Choice Health Security Act, initially contained an individual mandate with a penalty provision;[&#8202;](#ref-back-134-0)[\[134\]](#refnote-134) however, author [Don Nickles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Don_Nickles "Don Nickles") subsequently removed the mandate, stating, "government should not compel people to buy health insurance".[&#8202;](#ref-back-135-0)[\[135\]](#refnote-135) At the time of these proposals, Republicans did not raise constitutional issues; Mark Pauly, who helped develop a proposal that included an individual mandate for [George H. W. Bush](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_H._W._Bush "George H. W. Bush"), remarked, "I don't remember that being raised at all. The way it was viewed by the Congressional Budget Office in 1994 was, effectively, as a tax."[&#8202;](#ref-back-forbes1-1)[\[124\]](#refnote-forbes1)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/33/P112912PS-0444_-_President_Barack_Obama_and_Mitt_Romney_in_the_Oval_Office_-_crop.jpg/500px-P112912PS-0444_-_President_Barack_Obama_and_Mitt_Romney_in_the_Oval_Office_-_crop.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:P112912PS-0444_-_President_Barack_Obama_and_Mitt_Romney_in_the_Oval_Office_-_crop.jpg) Mitt Romney's [Massachusetts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massachusetts "Massachusetts") went from 90% of its residents insured to 98%, the highest rate in the nation.[&#8202;](#ref-back-136-0)[\[136\]](#refnote-136)

In 2006, [an insurance expansion bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massachusetts_health_care_reform "Massachusetts health care reform") was enacted at the state level in Massachusetts. The bill contained both an individual mandate and an [insurance exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_insurance_marketplace "Health insurance marketplace"). Republican Governor [Mitt Romney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mitt_Romney "Mitt Romney") used a line-item veto on some provisions, and the Democratic legislature overrode some of his changes (including the mandate).[&#8202;](#ref-back-governorromney-0)[\[137\]](#refnote-governorromney) Romney's implementation of the ['Health Connector' exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massachusetts_health_care_reform#Commonwealth_Health_Insurance_Connector_Authority "Massachusetts health care reform") and individual mandate in Massachusetts was at first lauded by Republicans. During [Romney's 2008 presidential campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mitt_Romney_presidential_campaign,_2008 "Mitt Romney presidential campaign, 2008"), Senator [Jim DeMint](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_DeMint "Jim DeMint") praised Romney's ability to "take some good conservative ideas, like private health insurance, and apply them to the need to have everyone insured". Romney said of the individual mandate: "I'm proud of what we've done. If Massachusetts succeeds in implementing it, then that will be the model for the nation."[&#8202;](#ref-back-new-yorker-lizza-0)[\[138\]](#refnote-new-yorker-lizza)

In 2007 Republican Senator [Bob Bennett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bob_Bennett_(politician) "Bob Bennett (politician)") and Democratic Senator [Ron Wyden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ron_Wyden "Ron Wyden") introduced the [Healthy Americans Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Healthy_Americans_Act "Healthy Americans Act"), which featured an individual mandate and state-based, [regulated insurance markets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_insurance_marketplace "Health insurance marketplace") called "State Health Help Agencies".[&#8202;](#ref-back-new-yorker-klein-1)[\[128\]](#refnote-new-yorker-klein)[&#8202;](#ref-back-new-yorker-lizza-1)[\[138\]](#refnote-new-yorker-lizza) The bill attracted bipartisan support, but died in committee. Many of its [sponsors and co-sponsors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Healthy_Americans_Act#Sponsors_and_co-sponsors "Healthy Americans Act") remained in Congress during the 2008 healthcare debate.[&#8202;](#ref-back-S.334summary-0)[\[139\]](#refnote-S.334summary)

By 2008 many Democrats were considering this approach as the basis for healthcare reform. Experts said the legislation that eventually emerged from Congress in 2009 and 2010 bore similarities to the 2007 bill[&#8202;](#ref-back-kaiserhealthnews1993-1)[\[131\]](#refnote-kaiserhealthnews1993) and that it took ideas from the Massachusetts reforms.[&#8202;](#ref-back-140-0)[\[140\]](#refnote-140)

### Academic foundation

A driving force behind Obama's healthcare reform was [Peter Orszag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_R._Orszag "Peter R. Orszag"), Director of the [Office of Management and Budget](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Office_of_Management_and_Budget "Office of Management and Budget").[&#8202;](#ref-back-141-0)[\[141\]](#refnote-141) Obama called Orszag his "healthcare czar" because of his knowledge of healthcare reform.[&#8202;](#ref-back-142-0)[\[142\]](#refnote-142) Orszag had previously been director of the [Congressional Budget Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congressional_Budget_Office "Congressional Budget Office"), and under his leadership the agency had focused on using cost analysis to create an affordable and effective approach to health care reform. Orszag claimed that healthcare reform became Obama's top agenda item because he wanted it to be his legacy.[&#8202;](#ref-back-143-0)[\[143\]](#refnote-143) According to an article by Ryan Lizza in _The New Yorker_, the core of "the Obama budget is Orszag's belief \[&#8202;](#ref-back-144-0)[in\]...a government empowered with research on the most effective medical treatments". Obama bet "his presidency on Orszag's thesis of comparative effectiveness."[\[144\]](#refnote-144) Orszag's policies were influenced by an article in _The Annals of Internal Medicine_[&#8202;](#ref-back-145-0)[\[145\]](#refnote-145) co-authored by Elliott S. Fisher, David Wennberg and others. The article presented strong evidence based on the co-authors' research that numerous procedures, therapies and tests were being delivered with scant evidence of their medical value. If those procedures and tests could be eliminated, this evidence suggested, medical costs might provide the savings to give healthcare to the uninsured population.[&#8202;](#ref-back-146-0)[\[146\]](#refnote-146) After reading a _New Yorker_ article that used the "Dartmouth findings"[&#8202;](#ref-back-147-0)[\[147\]](#refnote-147) to compare two counties in Texas with enormous variations in Medicare costs using hard data, Obama directed that his entire staff read it.[&#8202;](#ref-back-148-0)[\[148\]](#refnote-148) More than anything else, the Dartmouth data intrigued Obama[&#8202;](#ref-back-149-0)[\[149\]](#refnote-149) since it gave him an academic rationale for reshaping medicine.[&#8202;](#ref-back-150-0)[\[150\]](#refnote-150)

The concept of comparing the effectiveness of healthcare options based on hard data ("comparative effectiveness" and "evidence-based medicine") was pioneered by John E. Wennberg, founder of [The Dartmouth Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Dartmouth_Institute_for_Health_Policy_and_Clinical_Practice "The Dartmouth Institute for Health Policy and Clinical Practice"), co-founder of The Foundation for Informed Medical Decision Making and senior advisor to Health Dialog Inc., a venture that he and his researchers created to help insurers implement the Dartmouth findings.

### Healthcare debate, 2008–10

See also: [Health care reforms proposed during the Obama administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_care_reforms_proposed_during_the_Obama_administration "Health care reforms proposed during the Obama administration")

Healthcare reform was a major topic during the [2008 Democratic presidential primaries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Democratic_presidential_primaries "2008 Democratic presidential primaries"). As the race narrowed, attention focused on the plans presented by the two leading candidates, [Hillary Clinton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hillary_Clinton "Hillary Clinton") and the eventual nominee, [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama "Barack Obama"). Each candidate proposed a plan to cover the approximately 45million Americans estimated to not have health insurance at some point each year. Clinton's proposal would have required all Americans to obtain coverage (in effect, an individual mandate), while Obama's proposal provided a [subsidy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subsidy "Subsidy") without a mandate.[&#8202;](#ref-back-promise-0)[\[151\]](#refnote-promise)[&#8202;](#ref-back-152-0)[\[152\]](#refnote-152)

During the [general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_United_States_presidential_election "2008 United States presidential election"), Obama said fixing healthcare would be one of his top four priorities as president.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Sep2008FirstPresidentialDebate-0)[\[153\]](#refnote-Sep2008FirstPresidentialDebate) Obama and his opponent, Senator [John McCain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_McCain "John McCain"), both proposed health insurance reforms, though their plans differed. McCain proposed tax credits for health insurance purchased in the individual market, which was estimated to reduce the number of uninsured people by about 2million by 2018. Obama proposed private and public group insurance, income-based subsidies, consumer protections, and expansions of Medicaid and SCHIP, which was estimated at the time to reduce the number of uninsured people by 33.9 million by 2018 at a higher cost.[&#8202;](#ref-back-154-0)[\[154\]](#refnote-154)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cf/Joint_blog_close_PS-0774.jpg/500px-Joint_blog_close_PS-0774.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Joint_blog_close_PS-0774.jpg) President Obama [addressing Congress regarding healthcare reform](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama_speech_to_joint_session_of_Congress,_September_2009 "Barack Obama speech to joint session of Congress, September 2009"), September 9, 2009

Obama announced to a joint session of Congress in February 2009 his intent to work with Congress to construct a plan for healthcare reform.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Feb2009JointSessionAddress-0)[\[155\]](#refnote-Feb2009JointSessionAddress)[&#8202;](#ref-back-reuterstimeline-0)[\[156\]](#refnote-reuterstimeline) By July, a series of bills were approved by committees within the [House of Representatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_House_of_Representatives "United States House of Representatives").[&#8202;](#ref-back-EdLaborJul2009-0)[\[157\]](#refnote-EdLaborJul2009) On the Senate side, from June to September, the [Senate Finance Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Senate_Committee_on_Finance "United States Senate Committee on Finance") held a series of 31 meetings to develop a proposal. This group—in particular, Democrats [Max Baucus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Max_Baucus "Max Baucus"), [Jeff Bingaman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeff_Bingaman "Jeff Bingaman") and [Kent Conrad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kent_Conrad "Kent Conrad"), along with Republicans [Mike Enzi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Enzi "Mike Enzi"), [Chuck Grassley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chuck_Grassley "Chuck Grassley") and [Olympia Snowe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olympia_Snowe "Olympia Snowe")—met for more than 60 hours, and the principles they discussed, in conjunction with the other committees, became the foundation of a Senate bill.[&#8202;](#ref-back-158-0)[\[158\]](#refnote-158)[&#8202;](#ref-back-159-0)[\[159\]](#refnote-159)\[ _[new archival link needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:Archiving_a_source "Help:Archiving a source")_\][&#8202;](#ref-back-160-0)[\[160\]](#refnote-160)

Congressional Democrats and health policy experts, such as [MIT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MIT "MIT") economics professor [Jonathan Gruber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jonathan_Gruber_(economist) "Jonathan Gruber (economist)")[&#8202;](#ref-back-GruberBio2-0)[\[161\]](#refnote-GruberBio2) and [David Cutler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Cutler "David Cutler"), argued that [guaranteed issue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guaranteed_issue "Guaranteed issue") would require both [community rating](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Community_rating "Community rating") and an individual mandate to ensure that [adverse selection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adverse_selection "Adverse selection") or ["free riding"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_rider_problem "Free rider problem") would not result in an [insurance "death spiral"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_spiral_(insurance) "Death spiral (insurance)").[&#8202;](#ref-back-HowTheyDidIt-0)[\[162\]](#refnote-HowTheyDidIt) They chose this approach after concluding that [filibuster-proof support](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supermajority#United_States "Supermajority") in the Senate was not present for more progressive plans such as [single-payer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Single-payer_health_care "Single-payer health care"). By deliberately drawing on bipartisan ideas—the same basic outline was supported by former Senate Majority Leaders [Howard Baker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Howard_Baker "Howard Baker"), [Bob Dole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bob_Dole "Bob Dole"), [Tom Daschle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tom_Daschle "Tom Daschle") and [George J. Mitchell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_J._Mitchell "George J. Mitchell")—the bill's drafters hoped to garner the necessary votes.[&#8202;](#ref-back-163-0)[\[163\]](#refnote-163)[&#8202;](#ref-back-164-0)[\[164\]](#refnote-164)

However, following the incorporation of an individual mandate into the proposal, Republicans threatened to [filibuster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filibuster_in_the_United_States_Senate "Filibuster in the United States Senate") any bill that contained it.[&#8202;](#ref-back-forbes1-2)[\[124\]](#refnote-forbes1) [Senate Minority Leader](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Party_leaders_of_the_United_States_Senate "Party leaders of the United States Senate") [Mitch McConnell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mitch_McConnell "Mitch McConnell"), who led the Republican response, concluded Republicans should not support the bill.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ChaitLegislativeStrategy-0)[\[165\]](#refnote-ChaitLegislativeStrategy)

Republican senators, including those who had supported earlier proposals with a similar mandate, began to describe the mandate as "unconstitutional". Journalist [Ezra Klein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ezra_Klein "Ezra Klein") wrote in _[The New Yorker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_New_Yorker "The New Yorker")_, "a policy that once enjoyed broad support within the Republican Party suddenly faced unified opposition."[&#8202;](#ref-back-new-yorker-klein-2)[\[128\]](#refnote-new-yorker-klein)

The reform debate also attracted focused attention from [lobbyists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lobbying "Lobbying"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-166-0)[\[166\]](#refnote-166) including pharmaceutical and healthcare insurance company advocacy, to assure continued opposition from all Republicans and even from among more [conservative Democrats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_Dog_Coalition "Blue Dog Coalition"), who had not fully supported the public option in earlier proposals.[&#8202;](#ref-back-167-0)[\[167\]](#refnote-167)[&#8202;](#ref-back-168-0)[\[168\]](#refnote-168)[&#8202;](#ref-back-169-0)[\[169\]](#refnote-169)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/07/9.12_tea_party_in_DC.jpg/500px-9.12_tea_party_in_DC.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:9.12_tea_party_in_DC.jpg) Tea Party protesters at the [Taxpayer March on Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taxpayer_March_on_Washington "Taxpayer March on Washington"), September 12, 2009

During the August 2009 summer congressional recess, many members went back to their districts and held town hall meetings on the proposals. The nascent [Tea Party movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tea_Party_movement "Tea Party movement") organized protests and many [conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservatism_in_the_United_States "Conservatism in the United States") groups and individuals attended the meetings to oppose the proposed reforms.[&#8202;](#ref-back-reuterstimeline-1)[\[156\]](#refnote-reuterstimeline) Threats were made against members of Congress over the course of the debate.[&#8202;](#ref-back-WashPost-0)[\[170\]](#refnote-WashPost)

In September 2009 [Obama delivered another speech to a joint session of Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama_speech_to_joint_session_of_Congress,_September_2009 "Barack Obama speech to joint session of Congress, September 2009") supporting the negotiations.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Sep2009JointAddress-0)[\[171\]](#refnote-Sep2009JointAddress) On November 7, the House of Representatives passed the [Affordable Health Care for America Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Affordable_Health_Care_for_America_Act "Affordable Health Care for America Act") on a 220–215 vote and forwarded it to the Senate for passage.[&#8202;](#ref-back-reuterstimeline-2)[\[156\]](#refnote-reuterstimeline)

#### Senate

The Senate began work on its own proposals while the House was still working. The [United States Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Constitution "United States Constitution") requires all revenue-related bills to originate in the House.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Const-Revenue-0)[\[172\]](#refnote-Const-Revenue) To formally comply with this requirement, the Senate repurposed H.R. 3590, a bill regarding housing tax changes for service members.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ServicemembersHomeOwnershipTaxAct-0)[\[173\]](#refnote-ServicemembersHomeOwnershipTaxAct) It had been passed by the House as a revenue-related modification to the [Internal Revenue Code](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internal_Revenue_Code "Internal Revenue Code"). The bill became the Senate's vehicle for its healthcare reform proposal, discarding the bill's original content.[&#8202;](#ref-back-174-0)[\[174\]](#refnote-174) The bill ultimately incorporated elements of proposals that were reported favorably by the Senate [Health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Senate_Committee_on_Health,_Education,_Labor,_and_Pensions "United States Senate Committee on Health, Education, Labor, and Pensions") and [Finance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Senate_Committee_on_Finance "United States Senate Committee on Finance") committees. With the Republican Senate minority vowing to [filibuster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filibuster_in_the_United_States_Senate "Filibuster in the United States Senate"), 60 votes would be necessary to pass the Senate.[&#8202;](#ref-back-175-0)[\[175\]](#refnote-175) At the start of the [111th Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/111th_United_States_Congress#Party_summary "111th United States Congress"), Democrats had 58 votes. The [Minnesota Senate election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Senate_election_in_Minnesota,_2008 "United States Senate election in Minnesota, 2008") was ultimately won by Democrat [Al Franken](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Franken "Al Franken"), making 59. [Arlen Specter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arlen_Specter "Arlen Specter") switched to the Democratic party in April 2009, giving them 60 seats, enough to end a filibuster.

Negotiations were undertaken attempting to satisfy moderate Democrats and to bring Republican senators aboard; particular attention was given to Republicans Bennett, Enzi, Grassley and Snowe.

After the Finance Committee vote on October 15, negotiations turned to moderate Democrats. [Senate Majority Leader](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senate_Majority_Leader "Senate Majority Leader") [Harry Reid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harry_Reid "Harry Reid") focused on satisfying centrists. The holdouts came down to [Joe Lieberman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joe_Lieberman "Joe Lieberman") of Connecticut, an independent who caucused with Democrats, and conservative Nebraska Democrat [Ben Nelson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ben_Nelson "Ben Nelson"). Lieberman's demand that the bill not include a [public option](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_health_insurance_option "Public health insurance option")[&#8202;](#ref-back-HowTheyDidIt-1)[\[162\]](#refnote-HowTheyDidIt)[&#8202;](#ref-back-HackerReform-0)[\[176\]](#refnote-HackerReform) was met,[&#8202;](#ref-back-PublicOption-0)[\[177\]](#refnote-PublicOption) although supporters won various concessions, including allowing state-based public options such as Vermont's failed [Green Mountain Care](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Mountain_Care "Green Mountain Care").[&#8202;](#ref-back-PublicOption-1)[\[177\]](#refnote-PublicOption)[&#8202;](#ref-back-178-0)[\[178\]](#refnote-178)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/92/111th_Congress_1st_session_Senate_roll_call_396.svg/960px-111th_Congress_1st_session_Senate_roll_call_396.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:111th_Congress_1st_session_Senate_roll_call_396.svg) **Senate vote by state**

Democratic yes (58)

Independent yes (2)

Republican no (39)

Republican not voting (1)

The White House and Reid addressed Nelson's concerns[&#8202;](#ref-back-179-0)[\[179\]](#refnote-179) during a 13-hour negotiation with two concessions: a compromise on [abortion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abortion_debate "Abortion debate"), modifying the language of the bill "to give states the right to prohibit coverage of abortion within their own insurance exchanges", which would require consumers to pay for the procedure out of pocket if the state so decided; and an amendment to offer a higher rate of [Medicaid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicaid "Medicaid") reimbursement for Nebraska.[&#8202;](#ref-back-reuterstimeline-3)[\[156\]](#refnote-reuterstimeline)[&#8202;](#ref-back-180-0)[\[180\]](#refnote-180) The latter half of the compromise was derisively termed the "Cornhusker Kickback"[&#8202;](#ref-back-181-0)[\[181\]](#refnote-181) and was later removed.

On December 23, the Senate voted 60–39 to end debate on the bill: a [cloture vote](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cloture#United_States "Cloture") to end the [filibuster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filibuster_in_the_United_States_Senate "Filibuster in the United States Senate").[&#8202;](#ref-back-182-0)[\[182\]](#refnote-182) The bill then passed, also 60–39, on December 24, 2009, with all Democrats and two independents voting for it, and all Republicans against (except [Jim Bunning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Bunning "Jim Bunning"), who did not vote).[&#8202;](#ref-back-USS_RC_2009-0)[\[183\]](#refnote-USS_RC_2009) The bill was endorsed by the [American Medical Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Medical_Association "American Medical Association") and [AARP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AARP "AARP").[&#8202;](#ref-back-184-0)[\[184\]](#refnote-184)

On January 19, 2010, [Massachusetts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massachusetts "Massachusetts") Republican [Scott Brown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scott_Brown_(politician) "Scott Brown (politician)") was [elected to the Senate in a special election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Senate_special_election_in_Massachusetts,_2010 "United States Senate special election in Massachusetts, 2010") to replace the recently deceased [Ted Kennedy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ted_Kennedy "Ted Kennedy"), having campaigned on giving the Republican minority the 41st vote needed to sustain Republican filibusters.[&#8202;](#ref-back-reuterstimeline-4)[\[156\]](#refnote-reuterstimeline)[&#8202;](#ref-back-185-0)[\[185\]](#refnote-185)[&#8202;](#ref-back-186-0)[\[186\]](#refnote-186) Additionally, the symbolic importance of losing Kennedy's [traditionally Democratic Massachusetts seat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Massachusetts#Politics "Massachusetts") made many Congressional Democrats concerned about the political cost of the bill.[&#8202;](#ref-back-187-0)[\[187\]](#refnote-187)[&#8202;](#ref-back-BillPassageOptions-0)[\[188\]](#refnote-BillPassageOptions)

#### House

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/54/111th_Congress_roll_call_165.svg/960px-111th_Congress_roll_call_165.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:111th_Congress_roll_call_165.svg) **House vote by congressional district**

Democratic yes (219)

Democratic no (34)

Republican no (178)

No representative seated (4)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9a/United_States_2024.svg/500px-United_States_2024.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:United_States_2024.svg) House vote by agreement.

Yes (219)

No (212)

No representative seated (4)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c2/Obamacare_2010byparty.svg/500px-Obamacare_2010byparty.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Obamacare_2010byparty.svg) House vote by party

Democratic yes (219)

Democratic no (34)

Republican no (178)

No representative seated (4)

With Democrats no longer able to get the 60 votes to break a filibuster in the Senate, [White House Chief of Staff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_House_Chief_of_Staff "White House Chief of Staff") [Rahm Emanuel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rahm_Emanuel "Rahm Emanuel") argued that Democrats should scale back to a less ambitious bill, but [House Speaker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Speaker_of_the_United_States_House_of_Representatives "Speaker of the United States House of Representatives") [Nancy Pelosi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nancy_Pelosi "Nancy Pelosi") pushed back, dismissing more moderate reform as "Kiddie Care".[&#8202;](#ref-back-nytimesjourney-0)[\[189\]](#refnote-nytimesjourney)[&#8202;](#ref-back-PoliticoPelosi-0)[\[190\]](#refnote-PoliticoPelosi)

Obama remained insistent on comprehensive reform. The news that [Anthem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthem_(company) "Anthem (company)") in [California](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/California "California") intended to raise premium rates for its patients by as much as 39% gave him new evidence of the need for reform.[&#8202;](#ref-back-nytimesjourney-1)[\[189\]](#refnote-nytimesjourney)[&#8202;](#ref-back-PoliticoPelosi-1)[\[190\]](#refnote-PoliticoPelosi) On February 22, he laid out a "Senate-leaning" proposal to consolidate the bills.[&#8202;](#ref-back-191-0)[\[191\]](#refnote-191) He held a meeting with both parties' leaders on February 25. The Democrats decided the House would pass the Senate's bill, to avoid another Senate vote.

House Democrats had expected to be able to negotiate changes [in a House–Senate conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_congressional_conference_committee "United States congressional conference committee") before passing a final bill. Since any bill that emerged from conference that differed from the Senate bill would have to pass the Senate over another Republican filibuster, most House Democrats agreed to pass the Senate bill on condition that it be amended by a subsequent bill.[&#8202;](#ref-back-BillPassageOptions-1)[\[188\]](#refnote-BillPassageOptions) They drafted the [Health Care and Education Reconciliation Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_Care_and_Education_Reconciliation_Act_of_2010 "Health Care and Education Reconciliation Act of 2010"), which could be passed by the [reconciliation process](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reconciliation_(United_States_Congress) "Reconciliation (United States Congress)").[&#8202;](#ref-back-nytimesjourney-2)[\[189\]](#refnote-nytimesjourney)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Reconciliationprimer-0)[\[192\]](#refnote-Reconciliationprimer)[&#8202;](#ref-back-538Paths218-0)[\[193\]](#refnote-538Paths218)

Per the [Congressional Budget Act of 1974](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congressional_Budget_and_Impoundment_Control_Act_of_1974 "Congressional Budget and Impoundment Control Act of 1974"), reconciliation cannot be subject to a [filibuster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filibuster_in_the_United_States_Senate "Filibuster in the United States Senate"). But reconciliation is [limited to budget changes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reconciliation_(United_States_Congress)#Byrd_Rule "Reconciliation (United States Congress)"), which is why the procedure was not used to pass ACA in the first place; the bill had inherently non-budgetary regulations.[&#8202;](#ref-back-194-0)[\[194\]](#refnote-194)[&#8202;](#ref-back-195-0)[\[195\]](#refnote-195) Although the already-passed Senate bill could not have been passed by reconciliation, most of House Democrats' demands were budgetary: "these changes—higher subsidy levels, different kinds of taxes to pay for them, nixing the Nebraska Medicaid deal—mainly involve taxes and spending. In other words, they're exactly the kinds of policies that are well-suited for reconciliation."[&#8202;](#ref-back-Reconciliationprimer-1)[\[192\]](#refnote-Reconciliationprimer)

The remaining obstacle was a pivotal group of [pro-life](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-abortion_movements "Anti-abortion movements") Democrats led by [Bart Stupak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bart_Stupak "Bart Stupak") who were initially reluctant to support the bill. The group found the possibility of federal funding for abortion significant enough to warrant opposition. The Senate bill had not included language that satisfied their concerns, but they could not address abortion in the reconciliation bill as it would be non-budgetary.
