Instead, Obama issued [Executive Order 13535](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Executive_Order_13535 "Executive Order 13535"), reaffirming the principles in the [Hyde Amendment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyde_Amendment "Hyde Amendment") to continue banning the use of federal funds for abortion.[&#8202;](#ref-back-EO13535-0)[\[196\]](#refnote-EO13535)[&#8202;](#ref-back-197-0)[\[197\]](#refnote-197) This won the support of Stupak and members of his group and assured the bill's passage.[&#8202;](#ref-back-538Paths218-1)[\[193\]](#refnote-538Paths218)[&#8202;](#ref-back-198-0)[\[198\]](#refnote-198) The House passed the Senate bill with a 219–212 vote on March 21, 2010, with 34 Democrats and all 178 Republicans voting against it.[&#8202;](#ref-back-USH_RC_2010-0)[\[199\]](#refnote-USH_RC_2010) It passed the [second bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_Care_and_Education_Reconciliation_Act_of_2010 "Health Care and Education Reconciliation Act of 2010"), by 220–211, the same day (with the Senate passing this bill via reconciliation by 56-43 a few days later). The day after the passage of ACA, March 22, Republicans introduced legislation to repeal it.[&#8202;](#ref-back-PelosiSawyer-0)[\[200\]](#refnote-PelosiSawyer) Obama signed ACA into law on March 23, 2010.[&#8202;](#ref-back-24health-1)[\[18\]](#refnote-24health)

### Post-enactment

Since passage, Republicans have voted to repeal all or parts of the Affordable Care Act more than sixty times.[&#8202;](#ref-back-201-0)[\[201\]](#refnote-201)

The [Tax Cuts and Jobs Act of 2017](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tax_Cuts_and_Jobs_Act_of_2017 "Tax Cuts and Jobs Act of 2017") eliminated the fine for violating the individual mandate, starting in 2019. (The requirement itself is still in effect.)[&#8202;](#ref-back-auto-1)[\[58\]](#refnote-auto) In 2019 Congress repealed the so-called "Cadillac" tax on health insurance benefits, an excise tax on medical devices, and the Health Insurance Tax.[&#8202;](#ref-back-auto1-1)[\[98\]](#refnote-auto1)

The [American Rescue Plan Act of 2021](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Rescue_Plan_Act_of_2021 "American Rescue Plan Act of 2021"), enacted during the [COVID-19 pandemic in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_the_United_States "COVID-19 pandemic in the United States"), expanded subsidies for marketplace health plans. A continuation of these subsidies was introduced as part of the [Inflation Reduction Act of 2022](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inflation_Reduction_Act_of_2022 "Inflation Reduction Act of 2022").

On December 11, 2025, 48 Republican senators voted to block a bill to extend the subsidies. The ( [51—48](https://www.senate.gov/legislative/LIS/roll_call_lists/roll_call_vote_cfm.cfm?congress=119&session=1&vote=00644)) vote was less than the 60 votes [needed to proceed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cloture "Cloture") to extend the subsidies.[&#8202;](#ref-back-202-0)[\[202\]](#refnote-202) On [New Year's Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Year's_Day "New Year's Day") 2026, the Inflation Reduction Act's enhanced subsidies expired.[&#8202;](#ref-back-203-0)[\[203\]](#refnote-203)[&#8202;](#ref-back-204-0)[\[204\]](#refnote-204)[&#8202;](#ref-back-205-0)[\[205\]](#refnote-205)

## Impact

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4e/Health_Insurance_Coverage_in_the_U.S._2016_-_v1.png/1280px-Health_Insurance_Coverage_in_the_U.S._2016_-_v1.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Health_Insurance_Coverage_in_the_U.S._2016_-_v1.png) U.S. health insurance coverage by source in 2016. CBO estimated ACA/Obamacare was responsible for 23 million persons covered via exchanges and Medicaid expansion.[&#8202;](#ref-back-CBO_Subsidy2016-1)[\[5\]](#refnote-CBO_Subsidy2016)[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/93/ACA_Panel_Chart_v1.png/1280px-ACA_Panel_Chart_v1.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:ACA_Panel_Chart_v1.png) This chart illustrates several aspects of the Affordable Care Act, including number of persons covered, cost before and after subsidies, and public opinion.

### Coverage

See also: [Health insurance coverage in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_insurance_coverage_in_the_United_States "Health insurance coverage in the United States")

The law caused a significant reduction in the number and percentage of people without health insurance. The CDC reported that the percentage of people without health insurance fell from 16.0% in 2010 to 8.9% from January to June 2016.[&#8202;](#ref-back-206-0)[\[206\]](#refnote-206) The uninsured rate dropped in every congressional district in the U.S. from 2013 to 2015.[&#8202;](#ref-back-207-0)[\[207\]](#refnote-207) The [Congressional Budget Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congressional_Budget_Office "Congressional Budget Office") reported in March 2016 that approximately 12 million people were covered by the exchanges (10 million of whom received subsidies) and 11 million added to Medicaid. Another million were covered by ACA's "Basic Health Program", for a total of 24 million.[&#8202;](#ref-back-CBO_Subsidy2016-2)[\[5\]](#refnote-CBO_Subsidy2016) CBO estimated that ACA would reduce the net number of uninsured by 22 million in 2016, using a slightly different computation for the above figures totaling ACA coverage of 26 million, less 4million for reductions in "employment-based coverage" and "non-group and other coverage".[&#8202;](#ref-back-CBO_Subsidy2016-3)[\[5\]](#refnote-CBO_Subsidy2016)

The [U.S. Department of Health and Human Services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Department_of_Health_and_Human_Services "United States Department of Health and Human Services") (HHS) estimated that 20.0 million adults (aged 18–64) gained healthcare coverage via ACA as of February 2016;[&#8202;](#ref-back-HHS_ASPE16-1)[\[6\]](#refnote-HHS_ASPE16) similarly, the [Urban Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_Institute "Urban Institute") found in 2016 that 19.2 million non-elderly Americans gained health insurance coverage from 2010 to 2015.[&#8202;](#ref-back-208-0)[\[208\]](#refnote-208) In 2016, CBO estimated the uninsured at approximately 27 million people, or around 10% of the population or 7–8% excluding undocumented immigrants.[&#8202;](#ref-back-CBO_Subsidy2016-4)[\[5\]](#refnote-CBO_Subsidy2016)

States that expanded Medicaid had a 7.3% uninsured rate on average in the first quarter of 2016, while those that did not had a 14.1% uninsured rate, among adults aged 18–64.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Urban_Q12016-0)[\[209\]](#refnote-Urban_Q12016) As of December 2016 32 states (including Washington DC) had adopted the Medicaid extension.[&#8202;](#ref-back-KFF-Medicaid-0)[\[210\]](#refnote-KFF-Medicaid)

A 2017 study found that the ACA reduced socioeconomic disparities in health care access.[&#8202;](#ref-back-211-0)[\[211\]](#refnote-211)

The Affordable Care Act reduced the percent of Americans between 18 and 64 who were uninsured from 22.3 percent in 2010 to 12.4 percent in 2016. About 21 million more people have coverage ten years after the enactment of the ACA.[&#8202;](#ref-back-212-0)[\[212\]](#refnote-212)[&#8202;](#ref-back-NYT20200323GoodnoughAbelsonetAl-0)[\[213\]](#refnote-NYT20200323GoodnoughAbelsonetAl) Ten years after its enactment studies showed that the ACA also had a positive effect on health and caused a reduction in mortality.[&#8202;](#ref-back-NYT20200323GoodnoughAbelsonetAl-1)[\[213\]](#refnote-NYT20200323GoodnoughAbelsonetAl)

### Taxes

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/71/Excise_taxes.jpg/960px-Excise_taxes.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Excise_taxes.jpg) Excise taxes percentage 2015

[Excise taxes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Excise_tax_in_the_United_States "Excise tax in the United States") from the Affordable Care Act raised $16.3 billion in [fiscal year](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiscal_year "Fiscal year") 2015\. $11.3 billion came from an excise tax placed directly on health insurers based on their market share. Annual excise taxes totaling $3 billion were levied on importers and manufacturers of prescription drugs.

The [Individual mandate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Individual_mandate "Individual mandate") tax was $695 per individual or $2,085 per family at a minimum, reaching as high as 2.5% of household income (whichever was higher). The tax was set to $0 beginning in 2019.[&#8202;](#ref-back-214-0)[\[214\]](#refnote-214)

In the fiscal year 2018, the individual and employer mandates yielded $4 billion each. Excise taxes on insurers and drug makers added $18 billion. Income tax surcharges produced 437 billion.[&#8202;](#ref-back-215-0)[\[215\]](#refnote-215)

ACA reduced income inequality measured after taxes, due to the income tax surcharges and subsidies.[&#8202;](#ref-back-216-0)[\[216\]](#refnote-216) CBO estimated that subsidies paid under the law in 2016 averaged $4,240 per person for 10 million individuals receiving them, roughly $42 billion. The tax subsidy for the employer market, was approximately $1,700 per person in 2016, or $266 billion total.[&#8202;](#ref-back-CBO_Subsidy2016-5)[\[5\]](#refnote-CBO_Subsidy2016)

### Insurance exchanges

Main article: [Health insurance marketplace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_insurance_marketplace "Health insurance marketplace")

As of August 2016, 15 states operated their own [health insurance marketplace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_insurance_marketplace "Health insurance marketplace"). Other states either used the federal exchange, or operated in partnership with or supported by the federal government.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:0-0)[\[217\]](#refnote-:0) By 2019, 12 states and Washington DC operated their own exchanges.[&#8202;](#ref-back-218-0)[\[218\]](#refnote-218)

### Medicaid expansion in practice

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/47/Medicaid_expansion_map_of_US._Affordable_Care_Act.svg/960px-Medicaid_expansion_map_of_US._Affordable_Care_Act.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Medicaid_expansion_map_of_US._Affordable_Care_Act.svg) [ACA Medicaid expansion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicaid_coverage_gap#Medicaid_expansion "Medicaid coverage gap") by state.[&#8202;](#ref-back-KFF-Medicaid-1)[\[210\]](#refnote-KFF-Medicaid)

Not adopted

Adopted

Implemented

As of December 2019, 37 states (including Washington DC) had adopted the Medicaid extension.[&#8202;](#ref-back-KFF-Medicaid-2)[\[210\]](#refnote-KFF-Medicaid) Those states that expanded Medicaid had a 7.3% uninsured rate on average in the first quarter of 2016, while the others had a 14.1% uninsured rate, among adults aged 18 to 64.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Urban_Q12016-1)[\[209\]](#refnote-Urban_Q12016) Following the Supreme Court ruling in 2012, which held that states would not lose Medicaid funding if they did not expand Medicaid under ACA, several states rejected the option. Over half the national uninsured population lived in those states.[&#8202;](#ref-back-NYT52413-0)[\[219\]](#refnote-NYT52413)

The [Centers for Medicare and Medicaid Services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centers_for_Medicare_and_Medicaid_Services "Centers for Medicare and Medicaid Services") (CMS) estimated that the cost of expansion was $6,366 per person for 2015, about 49 percent above previous estimates. An estimated 9to 10 million people had gained Medicaid coverage, mostly low-income adults.\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\] The [Kaiser Family Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaiser_Family_Foundation "Kaiser Family Foundation") estimated in October 2015 that 3.1 million additional people were not covered because of states that rejected the Medicaid expansion.[&#8202;](#ref-back-220-0)[\[220\]](#refnote-220)[&#8202;](#ref-back-221-0)[\[221\]](#refnote-221)

In many states income thresholds were significantly below 133% of the poverty line.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Kliff,_Sarah-0)[\[222\]](#refnote-Kliff,_Sarah) Many states did not make Medicaid available to childless adults at any income level.[&#8202;](#ref-back-HealthCare_Reform_Magazine-0)[\[223\]](#refnote-HealthCare_Reform_Magazine) Because subsidies on exchange insurance plans were not available to those below the poverty line, such individuals had no new options.[&#8202;](#ref-back-224-0)[\[224\]](#refnote-224)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Families_USA-0)[\[225\]](#refnote-Families_USA) For example, in Kansas, where only non-disabled adults with children and with an income below 32% of the poverty line were eligible for Medicaid, those with incomes from 32% to 100% of the poverty level ($6,250 to $19,530 for a family of three) were ineligible for both Medicaid and federal subsidies to buy insurance. Absent children, non-disabled adults were not eligible for Medicaid there.[&#8202;](#ref-back-NYT52413-1)[\[219\]](#refnote-NYT52413)

Studies of the impact of Medicaid expansion rejections calculated that up to 6.4 million people would have too much income for Medicaid but not qualify for exchange subsidies.[&#8202;](#ref-back-226-0)[\[226\]](#refnote-226) Several states argued that they could not afford the 10% contribution in 2020.[&#8202;](#ref-back-227-0)[\[227\]](#refnote-227)[&#8202;](#ref-back-CNNMedicaid-0)[\[228\]](#refnote-CNNMedicaid)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Medicaiddeal-0)[\[229\]](#refnote-Medicaiddeal) Some studies suggested rejecting the expansion would cost more due to increased spending on uncompensated emergency care that otherwise would have been partially paid for by Medicaid coverage,[&#8202;](#ref-back-230-0)[\[230\]](#refnote-230)[&#8202;](#ref-back-231-0)[\[231\]](#refnote-231)

A 2016 study found that residents of Kentucky and Arkansas, which both expanded Medicaid, were more likely to receive health care services and less likely to incur emergency room costs or have trouble paying their medical bills. Residents of Texas, which did not accept the Medicaid expansion, did not see a similar improvement during the same period.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Sommers-0)[\[232\]](#refnote-Sommers)[&#8202;](#ref-back-233-0)[\[233\]](#refnote-233) Kentucky opted for increased managed care, while Arkansas subsidized private insurance. Later Arkansas and Kentucky governors proposed reducing or modifying their programs. From 2013 to 2015, the uninsured rate dropped from 42% to 14% in Arkansas and from 40% to 9% in Kentucky, compared with 39% to 32% in Texas.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Sommers-1)[\[232\]](#refnote-Sommers)[&#8202;](#ref-back-234-0)[\[234\]](#refnote-234)

A 2016 [DHHS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Department_of_Health_and_Human_Services "United States Department of Health and Human Services") study found that states that expanded Medicaid had lower premiums on exchange policies, because they had fewer low-income enrollees, whose health on average is worse than that of those with higher income.[&#8202;](#ref-back-235-0)[\[235\]](#refnote-235)

In September 2019, the Census Bureau reported that states that expanded Medicaid under the ACA had considerably lower uninsured rates than states that did not. For example, for adults between 100% and 399% of poverty level, the uninsured rate in 2018 was 12.7% in expansion states and 21.2% in non-expansion states. Of the 14 states with uninsured rates of 10% or greater, 11 had not expanded Medicaid.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Census_2018-0)[\[236\]](#refnote-Census_2018) The drop in uninsured rates due to expanded Medicaid has broadened access to care among low-income adults, with post-ACA studies indicating an improvement in affordability, access to doctors, and usual sources of care.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ACA_low_income-0)[\[237\]](#refnote-ACA_low_income)

A study using national data from the Health Reform Monitoring Survey determined that unmet need due to cost and inability to pay medical bills significantly decreased among low-income (up to 138% FPL) and moderate-income (139-199% FPL) adults, with unmet need due to cost decreasing by approximately 11 percentage points among low-income adults by the second enrollment period.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ACA_low_income-1)[\[237\]](#refnote-ACA_low_income) Importantly, issues with cost-related unmet medical needs, skipped medications, paying medical bills, and annual out-of-pocket spending have been significantly reduced among low-income adults in Medicaid expansion states compared to non-expansion states.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ACA_low_income-2)[\[237\]](#refnote-ACA_low_income)

As well, expanded Medicaid has led to a 6.6% increase in physician visits by low-income adults, as well as increased usage of preventative care such as dental visits and cancer screenings among childless, low-income adults.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ACA_low_income-3)[\[237\]](#refnote-ACA_low_income) Improved health care coverage due to Medicaid expansion has been found in a variety of patient populations, such as adults with mental and substance use disorders, trauma patients, cancer patients, and people living with HIV.[&#8202;](#ref-back-trauma_medicaid-0)[\[238\]](#refnote-trauma_medicaid)[&#8202;](#ref-back-HIV_medicaid-0)[\[239\]](#refnote-HIV_medicaid)[&#8202;](#ref-back-ACC_surgery_cancer-0)[\[240\]](#refnote-ACC_surgery_cancer)[&#8202;](#ref-back-ACA_mental_2017-0)[\[241\]](#refnote-ACA_mental_2017) Compared to 2011–13, in 2014 there was a 5.4 percentage point reduction in the uninsured rate of adults with mental disorders (from 21.3% to 15.9%) and a 5.1 percentage point reduction in the uninsured rate of adults with substance use disorders (from 25.9% to 20.8%); with increases in coverage occurring primarily through Medicaid.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ACA_mental_2017-1)[\[241\]](#refnote-ACA_mental_2017) Use of mental health treatment increased by 2.1 percentage points, from 43% to 45.1%.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ACA_mental_2017-2)[\[241\]](#refnote-ACA_mental_2017)

Among trauma patients nationwide, the uninsured rate has decreased by approximately 50%.[&#8202;](#ref-back-trauma_medicaid-1)[\[238\]](#refnote-trauma_medicaid) Adult trauma patients in expansion states experienced a 13.7 percentage point reduction in uninsured rates compared to adult trauma patients in non-expansion states, and an accompanying 7.4 percentage point increase in discharge to rehabilitation.[&#8202;](#ref-back-242-0)[\[242\]](#refnote-242) Following Medicaid expansion and dependent coverage expansion, young adults hospitalized for acute traumatic injury in Maryland experienced a 60% increase in rehabilitation, 25% reduction in mortality, and a 29.8% reduction in failure-to-rescue.[&#8202;](#ref-back-243-0)[\[243\]](#refnote-243) Medicaid expansion's swift impact on cancer patients was demonstrated in a study using the National Cancer Institute's Surveillance, Epidemiology, and End Results (SEER) program that evaluated more than 850,000 patients diagnosed with breast, lung, colorectal, prostate cancer, or thyroid cancer from 2010 to 2014. The study found that a cancer diagnosis in 2014 was associated with a 1.9 percentage-point absolute and 33.5% relative decrease in uninsured rates compared to a diagnosis made between 2010 and 2013.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ACC_surgery_cancer-1)[\[240\]](#refnote-ACC_surgery_cancer) Another study, using Surveillance, Epidemiology, and End Results (SEER) Program data from 2010 to 2014, found that Medicaid expansion was associated with a 6.4% net increase in early stage (in situ, local, or regional) diagnoses of all cancers combined.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ACA_cancer_10yr-0)[\[244\]](#refnote-ACA_cancer_10yr)

Data from the Centers for Disease and Prevention's (CDC) Medical Monitoring Project demonstrated that between 2009 and 2012, approximately 18% of people living with HIV (PLWH) who were actively receiving HIV treatment were uninsured[&#8202;](#ref-back-ACA_HIV-0)[\[245\]](#refnote-ACA_HIV) and that at least 40% of HIV-infected adults receiving treatment were insured through Medicaid or Medicare, programs they qualified for only once their disease was advanced enough to be covered as a disability under Social Security.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ACA_HIV-1)[\[245\]](#refnote-ACA_HIV) Expanded Medicaid coverage of PLWH has been positively associated with health outcomes such as viral suppression, retention of care, hospitalization rates, and morbidity at the time of hospitalization.[&#8202;](#ref-back-HIV_medicaid-1)[\[239\]](#refnote-HIV_medicaid) An analysis of Behavioral Risk Factor Surveillance System (BRFSS) survey data found a 2.8% annual increase in viral suppression rates among all PLWH from 2010 to 2015 due to Medicaid expansion.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ACA_HIV_90-0)[\[246\]](#refnote-ACA_HIV_90) As an early adopter of Medicaid expansion, Massachusetts found a 65% rate of viral suppression among all PLWH and an 85% rate among those retained in healthcare in 2014, both substantially higher than the national average.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ACA_HIV_90-1)[\[246\]](#refnote-ACA_HIV_90)

An analysis of hospital discharge data from 2012 to 2014 in four Medicaid expansion states and two non-expansion states revealed hospitalizations of uninsured PLWH fell from 13.7% to 5.5% in the four expansion states and rose from 14.5% to 15.7% in the two non-expansion states.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ACA_ryan_HIV_2019-0)[\[247\]](#refnote-ACA_ryan_HIV_2019) Importantly, uninsured PLWH were 40% more likely to die in the hospital than insured PLWH.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ACA_ryan_HIV_2019-1)[\[247\]](#refnote-ACA_ryan_HIV_2019) Other notable health outcomes associated with Medicaid expansion include improved glucose monitoring rates for patients with diabetes, better hypertension control, and reduced rates of major post-operative morbidity.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Medicaid_Expan_Sys_Rev-0)[\[248\]](#refnote-Medicaid_Expan_Sys_Rev)

A July 2019 study by the National Bureau of Economic Research (NBER) indicated that states enacting Medicaid expansion exhibited statistically significant reductions in mortality rates.[&#8202;](#ref-back-249-0)[\[249\]](#refnote-249) From that study, states that took Medicaid expansion "saved the lives of at least 19,200 adults aged 55 to 64 over the four-year period from 2014 to 2017."[&#8202;](#ref-back-CBPP-0)[\[250\]](#refnote-CBPP) Further, 15,600 older adults died prematurely in the states that did not enact Medicaid expansion in those years according to the NBER research. "The lifesaving impacts of Medicaid expansion are large: an estimated 39 to 64 percent reduction in annual mortality rates for older adults gaining coverage."[&#8202;](#ref-back-CBPP-1)[\[250\]](#refnote-CBPP)

Due to many states' failure to expand, many Democrats co-sponsored the proposed 2021 Cover Now Act that would allow county and municipal governments to fund Medicaid expansion.[&#8202;](#ref-back-251-0)[\[251\]](#refnote-251)

#### Gaps in expansion

Despite the significant increase in access to insurance coverage and healthcare services across the board, the ACA's Medicaid expansion has not fully addressed problems of economic equity. Critics argue that Medicaid expansion has not reduced [cost-sharing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cost_sharing "Cost sharing") by a significant margin, as the amount households paid out of pocket for healthcare over the past ten years (in the form of [deductibles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deductible "Deductible"), [co-payments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copayment "Copayment"), etc.) rose by 77%.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:02-0)[\[252\]](#refnote-:02) Additionally, 30% of providers deny Medicaid patients, which affects the accessibility of quality care.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:12-0)[\[253\]](#refnote-:12) This increase in denial may be in part because providers receive 62 cents from Medicaid for every dollar received from private insurers.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:12-1)[\[253\]](#refnote-:12) Studies on insurance rates show that economic inequality still persists: a significantly higher proportion of those with income greater than 100% but less than 200% of the [federal poverty level](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_poverty_level "Federal poverty level") were uninsured from 2010 to 2015 than of those with income greater than 200% of the federal poverty level.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:02-1)[\[252\]](#refnote-:02) This is exacerbated by the 2012 Supreme Court decision allowing states to opt out of Medicaid, since many of the states that have opted out have more vulnerable populations, with large numbers of minorities or low-income people.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:02-2)[\[252\]](#refnote-:02)

Medicaid patients have also reported receiving "second-class" treatment compared to privately insured patients, with longer wait times and lower quality of care.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:02-3)[\[252\]](#refnote-:02)

| ![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/bc/Flag_of_Wyoming.svg/60px-Flag_of_Wyoming.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)[Wyoming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wyoming "Wyoming") | No expansion | N/A | [HealthCare.gov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HealthCare.gov "HealthCare.gov") |  |

### Insurance costs

See also: [Health insurance costs in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_insurance_costs_in_the_United_States "Health insurance costs in the United States") and [Health care prices in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_care_prices_in_the_United_States "Health care prices in the United States")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/24/US_healthcare_cost_panel_v1.png/1280px-US_healthcare_cost_panel_v1.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:US_healthcare_cost_panel_v1.png)[&#8202;](#ref-back-cms-cost-0)[\[254\]](#refnote-cms-cost)

National health care expenditures rose faster than national income both before (2009–2013: 3.73%) and after (2014–2018: 4.82%) ACA's major provisions took effect.[&#8202;](#ref-back-255-0)[\[255\]](#refnote-255)[&#8202;](#ref-back-cms-cost-1)[\[254\]](#refnote-cms-cost) Premium prices rose considerably before and after. For example, a study published in 2016 found that the average requested 2017 premium increase among 40-year-old non-smokers was about 9percent, although Blue Cross Blue Shield proposed increases of 40 percent in Alabama and 60 percent in Texas.[&#8202;](#ref-back-256-0)[\[256\]](#refnote-256) However, some or all these costs were offset by tax credits. For example, the [Kaiser Family Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaiser_Family_Foundation "Kaiser Family Foundation") reported that for the second-lowest cost "Silver plan", a 40-year old non-smoker making $30,000 per year would pay effectively the same amount in 2017 as they did in 2016 (about $208/month) after the tax credit, despite a large increase in the list price. This was consistent nationally. In other words, the subsidies increased along with the premium price, fully offsetting the increases for subsidy-eligible enrollees.[&#8202;](#ref-back-257-0)[\[257\]](#refnote-257)

Premium cost increases in the employer market moderated after 2009. For example, healthcare premiums for those covered by employers rose by 69% from 2000 to 2005, but only 27% from 2010 to 2015,[&#8202;](#ref-back-Kaiser15-1)[\[7\]](#refnote-Kaiser15) with only a 3% increase from 2015 to 2016.[&#8202;](#ref-back-258-0)[\[258\]](#refnote-258) From 2008 to 2010 (before passage of ACA) health insurance premiums rose by an average of 10% per year.[&#8202;](#ref-back-259-0)[\[259\]](#refnote-259)

Several studies found that the [2008 financial crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_financial_crisis "2008 financial crisis") and accompanying [Great Recession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Recession "Great Recession") could not account for the entirety of the slowdown and that structural changes likely shared at least partial credit.[&#8202;](#ref-back-NYTLowrey1-0)[\[260\]](#refnote-NYTLowrey1)[&#8202;](#ref-back-paulryan-0)[\[261\]](#refnote-paulryan)[&#8202;](#ref-back-262-0)[\[262\]](#refnote-262)[&#8202;](#ref-back-263-0)[\[263\]](#refnote-263) A 2013 study estimated that changes to the health system had been responsible for about a quarter of the recent reduction in inflation.[&#8202;](#ref-back-264-0)[\[264\]](#refnote-264)\[ _[clarification needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Please_clarify "Wikipedia:Please clarify")_\] Paul Krawzak claimed that even if cost controls succeed in reducing the amount spent on healthcare, such efforts on their own may be insufficient to outweigh the long-term burden placed by demographic changes, particularly [the growth of the population on Medicare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicare_(United_States)#Costs_and_funding_challenges "Medicare (United States)").[&#8202;](#ref-back-265-0)[\[265\]](#refnote-265)

In a 2016 review, Barack Obama claimed that from 2010 through 2014 mean annual growth in real per-enrollee Medicare spending was negative, down from a mean of 4.7% per year from 2000 through 2005 and 2.4% per year from 2006 to 2010; similarly, mean real per-enrollee growth in private insurance spending was 1.1% per year over the period, compared with a mean of 6.5% from 2000 through 2005 and 3.4% from 2005 to 2010.[&#8202;](#ref-back-266-0)[\[266\]](#refnote-266)

#### Deductibles and co-payments

A contributing factor to premium cost moderation was that the insured faced higher [deductibles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deductible "Deductible"), [copayments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Copayment "Copayment") and out-of-pocket maximums. In addition, many employees chose to combine a [health savings account](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_savings_account "Health savings account") with higher deductible plans, making the net impact of ACA difficult to determine precisely.

For the group market (employer insurance), a 2016 survey found that:

- Deductibles grew 63% from 2011 to 2016, while premiums increased 19% and worker earnings grew by 11%.
- In 2016, 4 in 5 workers had an insurance deductible, which averaged $1,478. For firms with less than 200 employees, the deductible averaged $2,069.
- The percentage of workers with a deductible of at least $1,000 grew from 10% in 2006 to 51% in 2016. The 2016 figure dropped to 38% after taking employer contributions into account.[&#8202;](#ref-back-267-0)[\[267\]](#refnote-267)

For the non-group market, of which two-thirds are covered by ACA exchanges, a survey of 2015 data found that:

- 49% had individual deductibles of at least $1,500 ($3,000 for family), up from 36% in 2014.
- Many exchange enrollees qualify for cost-sharing subsidies that reduce their net deductible.
- While about 75% of enrollees were "very satisfied" or "somewhat satisfied" with their choice of doctors and hospitals, only 50% had such satisfaction with their annual deductible.
- While 52% of those covered by ACA exchanges felt "well protected" by their insurance, in the group market 63% felt that way.[&#8202;](#ref-back-268-0)[\[268\]](#refnote-268)

### Health outcomes

According to a 2014 study, ACA likely prevented an estimated 50,000 preventable patient deaths from 2010 to 2013.[&#8202;](#ref-back-269-0)[\[269\]](#refnote-269) Himmelstein and Woolhandler wrote in January 2017 that a rollback of ACA's Medicaid expansion alone would cause an estimated 43,956 deaths annually.[&#8202;](#ref-back-270-0)[\[270\]](#refnote-270)

According to the Kaiser Foundation, expanding Medicaid in the remaining states would cover up to 4.5 million persons.[&#8202;](#ref-back-271-0)[\[271\]](#refnote-271) A 2021 study found a significant decline in mortality rates in the states that opted in to the Medicaid expansion program compared with those states that did not do so. The study reported that states decisions' not to expand Medicaid resulted in approximately 15,600 excess deaths from 2014 through 2017.[&#8202;](#ref-back-272-0)[\[272\]](#refnote-272)[&#8202;](#ref-back-273-0)[\[273\]](#refnote-273)

Dependent Coverage Expansion (DCE) under the ACA has had a demonstrable effect on various health metrics of young adults, a group with a historically low level of insurance coverage and utilization of care.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ACA_DCE_2017-0)[\[274\]](#refnote-ACA_DCE_2017) Numerous studies have shown the target age group gained private health insurance relative to an older group after the policy was implemented, with an accompanying improvement in having a usual source of care, reduction in out-of-pocket costs of high-end medical expenditures, reduction in frequency of Emergency Department visits, 3.5% increase in hospitalizations and 9% increase in hospitalizations with a psychiatric diagnosis, 5.3% increase in utilizing specialty mental health care by those with a probable mental illness, 4% increase in reporting excellent mental health, and a 1.5-6.2% increase in reporting excellent physical health.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ACA_DCE_2017-1)[\[274\]](#refnote-ACA_DCE_2017) Studies have also found that DCE was associated with improvements in cancer prevention, detection, and treatment among young adult patients.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ACA_cancer_10yr-1)[\[244\]](#refnote-ACA_cancer_10yr)[&#8202;](#ref-back-ACA_cancer_YA-0)[\[275\]](#refnote-ACA_cancer_YA) A study of 10,010 women aged 18–26 identified through the 2008-12 National Health Interview Survey found that the likelihood of HPV vaccination initiation and completion increased by 7.7 and 5.8 percentage points respectively when comparing before and after October 1, 2010.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ACA_cancer_YA-1)[\[275\]](#refnote-ACA_cancer_YA) Another study using National Cancer Database (NCDB) data from 2007 to 2012 found a 5.5 percentage point decrease in late-stage (stages III/IV) cervical cancer diagnosis for women aged 21–25 after DCE, and an overall decrease of 7.3 percentage points in late-stage diagnosis compared to those aged 26–34.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ACA_cancer_10yr-2)[\[244\]](#refnote-ACA_cancer_10yr) A study using SEER Program data from 2007 to 2012 found a 2.7 percentage point increase in diagnosis at stage I disease for patients aged 19–25 compared with those aged 26–34 for all cancers combined.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ACA_cancer_10yr-3)[\[244\]](#refnote-ACA_cancer_10yr) Studies focusing on cancer treatment after DCE found a 12.8 percentage point increase in the receipt of fertility-sparing treatment among cervical cancer patients aged 21–25 and an overall increase of 13.4 percentage points compared to those aged 26–34, as well as an increased likelihood that patients aged 19–25 with stage IIB-IIIC colorectal cancer receive timely adjuvant chemotherapy compared to those aged 27–34.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ACA_cancer_10yr-4)[\[244\]](#refnote-ACA_cancer_10yr)

Two 2018 _[JAMA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/JAMA_(journal) "JAMA (journal)")_ studies found the Hospital Readmissions Reduction Program (HRRP) was associated with increased post-discharge mortality for patients hospitalized for heart failure and pneumonia.[&#8202;](#ref-back-276-0)[\[276\]](#refnote-276)[&#8202;](#ref-back-277-0)[\[277\]](#refnote-277)[&#8202;](#ref-back-278-0)[\[278\]](#refnote-278) A 2019 _JAMA_ study found that ACA decreased emergency department and hospital use by uninsured individuals.[&#8202;](#ref-back-279-0)[\[279\]](#refnote-279) Several studies have indicated that increased 30-day, 90-day, and 1-year post-discharge mortality of heart failure patients can be attributed to "gaming the system" through inappropriate triage systems in emergency departments, use of observation stays when admissions are warranted, and delay of readmission beyond the 30th day post-discharge, strategies that can reduce readmission rates at the expense of quality of care and patient survival.[&#8202;](#ref-back-280-0)[\[280\]](#refnote-280) The HRRP was also shown to disproportionately penalize safety-net hospitals that predominately serve low-income patients.[&#8202;](#ref-back-281-0)[\[281\]](#refnote-281) A 2020 study by Treasury Department economists in the _[Quarterly Journal of Economics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quarterly_Journal_of_Economics "Quarterly Journal of Economics")_ using a [randomized controlled trial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Randomized_controlled_trial "Randomized controlled trial") (the IRS sent letters to some taxpayers noting that they had paid a fine for not signing up for health insurance but not to other taxpayers) found that over two years, obtaining health insurance reduced mortality by 12 percent.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:6-0)[\[282\]](#refnote-:6)[&#8202;](#ref-back-283-0)[\[283\]](#refnote-283) The study concluded that the letters, sent to 3.9 million people, may have saved 700 lives.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:6-1)[\[282\]](#refnote-:6)

A 2020 _JAMA_ study found that Medicare expansion under the ACA was associated with reduced incidence of advanced-stage breast cancer, indicating that Medicaid accessibility led to early detection of breast cancer and higher survival rates.[&#8202;](#ref-back-284-0)[\[284\]](#refnote-284) Recent studies have also attributed to Medicaid expansion an increase in use of smoking cessation medications, cervical cancer screening, and colonoscopy, as well as an increase in the percentage of early-stage diagnosis of all cancers and the rate of cancer surgery for low-income patients.[&#8202;](#ref-back-medicaid_cancer_continu-0)[\[285\]](#refnote-medicaid_cancer_continu)[&#8202;](#ref-back-286-0)[\[286\]](#refnote-286) These studies include a 2.1% increase in the probability of smoking cessation in Medicaid expansion states compared to non-expansion states, a 24% increase in smoking cessation medication use due to increased Medicaid-financed smoking cessation prescriptions, a 27.7% increase in the rate of colorectal cancer screening in Kentucky following Medicaid expansion with an accompanying improvement in colorectal cancer survival, and a 3.4% increase in cancer incidence following Medicaid expansion that was attributed to an increase in early-stage diagnoses.[&#8202;](#ref-back-medicaid_cancer_continu-1)[\[285\]](#refnote-medicaid_cancer_continu)

Transition-of-care interventions and Alternative Payment Models under the ACA have also shown promise in improving health outcomes.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ACA_TOC_2017-0)[\[287\]](#refnote-ACA_TOC_2017)[&#8202;](#ref-back-ACA_APM_Ortho-0)[\[288\]](#refnote-ACA_APM_Ortho) Post-discharge provider appointment and telephone follow-up interventions have been shown to reduce 30-day readmission rates among general medical-surgical inpatients.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ACA_TOC_2017-1)[\[287\]](#refnote-ACA_TOC_2017) Reductions in 60, 90, and 180 post-discharge day readmission rates due to transition-of-care interventions have also been demonstrated, and a reduction in 30-day mortality has been suggested.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ACA_TOC_2017-2)[\[287\]](#refnote-ACA_TOC_2017) Total joint arthroplasty bundles as part of the Bundled Payments for Care Improvement initiative have been shown to reduce discharge to inpatient rehabilitation facilities and post-acute care facilities, decrease hospital length of stay by 18% without sacrificing quality of care, and reduce the rate of total joint arthroplasty readmissions, half of which were due to surgical complications.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ACA_APM_Ortho-1)[\[288\]](#refnote-ACA_APM_Ortho) The Hospital Value-Based Purchasing Program in Medicaid has also shown the potential to improve health outcomes, with early studies reporting positive and significant effects on total patient experience score, 30-day readmission rates, incidences of pneumonia and pressure ulcers, and 30-day mortality rates for pneumonia.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ACA_HVBP_2020-0)[\[289\]](#refnote-ACA_HVBP_2020) The patient-centered medical home (PCMH) payment and care model, a team-based approach to population health management that risk-stratifies patients and provides focused care management and outreach to high-risk patients, has been shown to improve diabetes outcomes.[&#8202;](#ref-back-APM_diabetes-0)[\[290\]](#refnote-APM_diabetes) A widespread PCMH demonstration program focusing on diabetes, known as the Chronic Care Initiative in the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania, found statistically significant improvements in A1C testing, LDL-C testing, nephropathy screening and monitoring, and eye examinations, with an accompanying reduction in all-cause emergency department visits, ambulatory care-sensitive emergency department visits, ambulatory visits to specialists, and a higher rate of ambulatory visits to primary care providers.[&#8202;](#ref-back-APM_diabetes-1)[\[290\]](#refnote-APM_diabetes) The ACA overall has improved coverage and care of diabetes, with a significant portion of the 3.5 million uninsured US adults aged 18–64 with diabetes in 2009-10 likely gaining coverage and benefits such as closure of the Medicaid Part D coverage gap for insulin.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ACA_diabetes-0)[\[291\]](#refnote-ACA_diabetes) 2.3 million of the approximately 4.6 million people aged 18–64 with undiagnosed diabetes in 2009–2010 may also have gained access to zero-cost preventative care due to section 2713 of the ACA, which prohibits cost sharing for United States Preventive Services Taskforce grade A or B recommended services, such as diabetes screenings.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ACA_diabetes-1)[\[291\]](#refnote-ACA_diabetes)

### Distributional impact

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/4/4c/1b-ACA_Distribution_in_2014.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail_unscaled)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:1b-ACA_Distribution_in_2014.png) The distributional impact of the Affordable Care Act (ACA or Obamacare) during 2014. ACA raised taxes mainly on the top 1% to fund approximately $600 in benefits on average for the bottom 40% of families.

In March 2018, the CBO reported that ACA had reduced income inequality in 2014, saying the law led the lowest and second quintiles (the bottom 40%) to receive an average of an additional $690 and $560 respectively while causing households in the top 1% to pay an additional $21,000 due mostly to the net investment income tax and the additional Medicare tax. The law placed relatively little burden on households in the top quintile (top 20%) outside of the top 1%.[&#8202;](#ref-back-CBO_Dist14-1)[\[10\]](#refnote-CBO_Dist14)

### Federal deficit

#### CBO estimates of revenue and impact on deficit

See also: [United States public debt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_public_debt "United States public debt") and [Provisions of the Patient Protection and Affordable Care Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Provisions_of_the_Patient_Protection_and_Affordable_Care_Act "Provisions of the Patient Protection and Affordable Care Act")

The CBO reported in multiple studies that ACA would reduce the deficit, and repealing it would increase the deficit, primarily because of the elimination of Medicare reimbursement cuts.[&#8202;](#ref-back-CBO50252-1)[\[8\]](#refnote-CBO50252)[&#8202;](#ref-back-CBO22077-1)[\[9\]](#refnote-CBO22077) The 2011 comprehensive CBO estimate projected a net deficit reduction of more than $200 billion during the 2012–2021 period:[&#8202;](#ref-back-CBO22077-2)[\[9\]](#refnote-CBO22077)[&#8202;](#ref-back-CBO43104-0)[\[292\]](#refnote-CBO43104) it calculated the law would result in $604 billion in [total outlays](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_spending "Government spending") offset by $813 billion in [total receipts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_revenue "Government revenue"), resulting in a $210 billion net deficit reduction.[&#8202;](#ref-back-CBO22077-3)[\[9\]](#refnote-CBO22077) The CBO separately predicted that while most of the spending provisions do not begin until 2014,[&#8202;](#ref-back-CBO-Pelosi-0)[\[293\]](#refnote-CBO-Pelosi)[&#8202;](#ref-back-RollCallCBO-0)[\[294\]](#refnote-RollCallCBO) revenue would exceed spending in those subsequent years.[&#8202;](#ref-back-CBO-Pelosi2-0)[\[295\]](#refnote-CBO-Pelosi2)\[ _[dead link](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Link_rot "Wikipedia:Link rot")_\] The CBO claimed the bill would "substantially reduce the growth of Medicare's payment rates for most services; impose an excise tax on insurance plans with relatively high premiums; and make various other changes to the federal tax code, Medicare, Medicaid, and other programs"[&#8202;](#ref-back-CBO-Reid-Dec2009-0)[\[296\]](#refnote-CBO-Reid-Dec2009)—ultimately extending the solvency of the [Medicare trust fund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicare_(United_States)#The_solvency_of_the_Medicare_HI_trust_fund "Medicare (United States)") by eight years.[&#8202;](#ref-back-297-0)[\[297\]](#refnote-297)

This estimate was made prior to the [Supreme Court's ruling](#Legal_challenges) that enabled states to [opt out of the Medicaid expansion](#Medicaid_expansion), thereby forgoing the related federal funding. The [CBO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congressional_Budget_Office "Congressional Budget Office") and [JCT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Congress_Joint_Committee_on_Taxation "United States Congress Joint Committee on Taxation") subsequently updated the budget projection, estimating the impact of the ruling would reduce the cost estimate of the insurance coverage provisions by $84 billion.[&#8202;](#ref-back-fewer-0)[\[298\]](#refnote-fewer)[&#8202;](#ref-back-CBO43472-0)[\[299\]](#refnote-CBO43472)[&#8202;](#ref-back-300-0)[\[300\]](#refnote-300)

The CBO in June 2015 forecast that repeal of ACA would increase the deficit between $137 billion and $353 billion over the 2016–2025 period, depending on the impact of macroeconomic [feedback](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dynamic_scoring "Dynamic scoring") effects. The CBO also forecast that repeal of ACA would likely cause an increase in GDP by an average of 0.7% in the period from 2021 to 2025, mainly by boosting the supply of labor.[&#8202;](#ref-back-CBO50252-2)[\[8\]](#refnote-CBO50252)

Although the CBO generally does not provide cost estimates beyond the 10-year budget projection period because of the degree of uncertainty involved in the projection, it decided to do so in this case at the request of lawmakers, and estimated a second decade deficit reduction of $1.2trillion.[&#8202;](#ref-back-CBO-Reid-Dec2009-1)[\[296\]](#refnote-CBO-Reid-Dec2009)[&#8202;](#ref-back-CNN-Mar18-0)[\[301\]](#refnote-CNN-Mar18) CBO predicted deficit reduction around a broad range of one-half percent of GDP over the 2020s while cautioning that "a wide range of changes could occur".[&#8202;](#ref-back-302-0)[\[302\]](#refnote-302)

In 2017 CBO estimated that repealing the individual mandate alone would reduce the 10-year deficit by $338 billion.[&#8202;](#ref-back-303-0)[\[303\]](#refnote-303)

#### Opinions on CBO projections

The CBO cost estimates were criticized because they excluded the effects of potential legislation that would increase Medicare payments by more than $200billion from 2010 to 2019.[&#8202;](#ref-back-304-0)[\[304\]](#refnote-304)\[ _[new archival link needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:Archiving_a_source "Help:Archiving a source")_\][&#8202;](#ref-back-305-0)[\[305\]](#refnote-305)[&#8202;](#ref-back-306-0)[\[306\]](#refnote-306) However, the so-called " [doc fix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medicare_Sustainable_Growth_Rate "Medicare Sustainable Growth Rate")" is a separate issue that would have existed with or without ACA.[&#8202;](#ref-back-307-0)[\[307\]](#refnote-307)[&#8202;](#ref-back-308-0)[\[308\]](#refnote-308)[&#8202;](#ref-back-309-0)[\[309\]](#refnote-309) The [Center on Budget and Policy Priorities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Center_on_Budget_and_Policy_Priorities "Center on Budget and Policy Priorities") objected that Congress had a good record of implementing Medicare savings. According to their study, Congress followed through on the implementation of the vast majority of provisions enacted in the past 20years to produce Medicare savings, although not the doc fix.[&#8202;](#ref-back-310-0)[\[310\]](#refnote-310)[&#8202;](#ref-back-311-0)[\[311\]](#refnote-311) The doc fix became obsolete in 2015 when the savings provision was eliminated, permanently removing that spending restraint.[&#8202;](#ref-back-312-0)[\[312\]](#refnote-312)

[Health economist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_economist "Health economist") [Uwe Reinhardt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uwe_Reinhardt "Uwe Reinhardt"), wrote, "The rigid, artificial rules under which the Congressional Budget Office must score proposed legislation unfortunately cannot produce the best unbiased forecasts of the likely fiscal impact of any legislation."[&#8202;](#ref-back-313-0)[\[313\]](#refnote-313) [Douglas Holtz-Eakin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Douglas_Holtz-Eakin "Douglas Holtz-Eakin") alleged that the bill would increase the deficit by $562billion because, he argued, it front-loaded revenue and back-loaded benefits.[&#8202;](#ref-back-314-0)[\[314\]](#refnote-314)

Scheiber and Cohn rejected critical assessments of the law's deficit impact, arguing that predictions were biased towards underestimating deficit reduction. They noted, for example, it is easier to account for the cost of definite levels of subsidies to specified numbers of people than to account for savings from [preventive healthcare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Preventive_medicine "Preventive medicine"), and that the CBO had a track record of overestimating costs and underestimating savings of health legislation;[&#8202;](#ref-back-CBOMethodology-0)[\[315\]](#refnote-CBOMethodology)[&#8202;](#ref-back-CBOTrackRecord-0)[\[316\]](#refnote-CBOTrackRecord) stating, "innovations in the delivery of medical care, like greater use of [electronic medical records](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electronic_medical_record "Electronic medical record")[&#8202;](#ref-back-317-0)[\[317\]](#refnote-317) and financial incentives for more coordination of care among doctors, would produce substantial savings while also slowing the relentless climb of medical expenses... But the CBO would not consider such savings in its calculations, because the innovations hadn't really been tried on such large scale or in concert with one another—and that meant there wasn't much hard data to prove the savings would materialize."[&#8202;](#ref-back-CBOMethodology-1)[\[315\]](#refnote-CBOMethodology)

In 2010 [David Walker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_M._Walker_(U.S._Comptroller_General) "David M. Walker (U.S. Comptroller General)") said the CBO estimates were not likely to be accurate, because they were based on the assumption that the law would not change.[&#8202;](#ref-back-deficit_david_walker_cbo_inaccurate-0)[\[318\]](#refnote-deficit_david_walker_cbo_inaccurate)

### Employer mandate and part-time work

Further informationon health insurance mandates: [Health insurance mandate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_insurance_mandate "Health insurance mandate")

The employer mandate applies to employers of more than fifty where health insurance is provided only to the full-time workers.[&#8202;](#ref-back-GPO-0)[\[319\]](#refnote-GPO) Critics claimed it created a [perverse incentive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perverse_incentive "Perverse incentive") to hire part-timers instead.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ChaitNotWreck-0)[\[320\]](#refnote-ChaitNotWreck)[&#8202;](#ref-back-KliffPartTime-0)[\[321\]](#refnote-KliffPartTime) However, between March 2010 and 2014, the number of part-time jobs declined by 230,000 while the number of full-time jobs increased by two million.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FORBES-0)[\[322\]](#refnote-FORBES)[&#8202;](#ref-back-BUREAU_OF_LABOR_AND_STATISTICS-0)[\[323\]](#refnote-BUREAU_OF_LABOR_AND_STATISTICS) In the public sector full-time jobs turned into part-time jobs much more than in the private sector.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FORBES-1)[\[322\]](#refnote-FORBES)[&#8202;](#ref-back-324-0)[\[324\]](#refnote-324) A 2016 study found only limited evidence that ACA had increased part-time employment.[&#8202;](#ref-back-325-0)[\[325\]](#refnote-325)

Several businesses and the state of Virginia added a 29-hour-a-week cap for their part-time employees,[&#8202;](#ref-back-326-0)[\[326\]](#refnote-326)\[ _[unreliable source?](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Reliable_sources "Wikipedia:Reliable sources")_\][&#8202;](#ref-back-327-0)[\[327\]](#refnote-327)\[ _[unreliable source?](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Reliable_sources "Wikipedia:Reliable sources")_\] to reflect the 30-hour-or-more definition for full-time worker.[&#8202;](#ref-back-GPO-1)[\[319\]](#refnote-GPO) As of 2013, few companies had shifted their workforce towards more part-time hours (4% in a survey from the [Federal Reserve Bank of Minneapolis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Reserve_Bank_of_Minneapolis "Federal Reserve Bank of Minneapolis")).[&#8202;](#ref-back-KliffPartTime-1)[\[321\]](#refnote-KliffPartTime) Trends in working hours[&#8202;](#ref-back-NPRPartTime-0)[\[328\]](#refnote-NPRPartTime) and the recovery from the [Great Recession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Recession "Great Recession") correlate with the shift from part-time to full-time work.[&#8202;](#ref-back-329-0)[\[329\]](#refnote-329)[&#8202;](#ref-back-330-0)[\[330\]](#refnote-330) Other confounding impacts include that health insurance helps attract and retain employees, increases productivity and reduces absenteeism; and lowers corresponding training and administration costs from a smaller, more stable workforce.[&#8202;](#ref-back-KliffPartTime-2)[\[321\]](#refnote-KliffPartTime)[&#8202;](#ref-back-NPRPartTime-1)[\[328\]](#refnote-NPRPartTime)[&#8202;](#ref-back-331-0)[\[331\]](#refnote-331) Relatively few firms employ over 50 employees[&#8202;](#ref-back-KliffPartTime-3)[\[321\]](#refnote-KliffPartTime) and more than 90% of them already offered insurance.[&#8202;](#ref-back-CohnDelay-0)[\[332\]](#refnote-CohnDelay)

Most policy analysts (both right and left) were critical of the employer mandate provision.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ChaitNotWreck-1)[\[320\]](#refnote-ChaitNotWreck)[&#8202;](#ref-back-CohnDelay-1)[\[332\]](#refnote-CohnDelay) They argued that the perverse incentives regarding part-time hours, even if they did not change existing plans, were real and harmful;[&#8202;](#ref-back-CBPPEmpMand.-0)[\[333\]](#refnote-CBPPEmpMand.)[&#8202;](#ref-back-MandateRepeal-0)[\[334\]](#refnote-MandateRepeal) that the raised [marginal cost](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marginal_cost "Marginal cost") of the 50th worker for businesses could limit companies' growth;[&#8202;](#ref-back-335-0)[\[335\]](#refnote-335) that the costs of reporting and administration were not worth the costs of maintaining employer plans;[&#8202;](#ref-back-CBPPEmpMand.-1)[\[333\]](#refnote-CBPPEmpMand.)[&#8202;](#ref-back-MandateRepeal-1)[\[334\]](#refnote-MandateRepeal) and noted that the employer mandate was not essential to maintain adequate risk pools.[&#8202;](#ref-back-336-0)[\[336\]](#refnote-336)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Mandate3-0)[\[337\]](#refnote-Mandate3) The provision generated vocal opposition from business interests and some unions who were not granted exemptions.[&#8202;](#ref-back-MandateRepeal-2)[\[334\]](#refnote-MandateRepeal)[&#8202;](#ref-back-WSJ-0)[\[338\]](#refnote-WSJ)

### Hospitals

From the start of 2010 to November 2014, 43 hospitals in [rural areas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rural_area#United_States "Rural area") closed. Critics claimed the new law had caused these closures. Many rural hospitals were built using funds from the 1946 [Hill–Burton Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hill%E2%80%93Burton_Act "Hill–Burton Act"). Some of these hospitals reopened as other medical facilities, but only a small number operated [emergency rooms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emergency_department "Emergency department") (ER) or [urgent care](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urgent_care "Urgent care") centers.[&#8202;](#ref-back-339-0)[\[339\]](#refnote-339)

Between January 2010 and 2015, a quarter of ER doctors said they had seen a major surge in patients, while nearly half had seen a smaller increase. Seven in ten ER doctors claimed they lacked the resources to deal with large increases in the number of patients. The biggest factor in the increased number of ER patients was insufficient primary care providers to handle the larger number of insured.[&#8202;](#ref-back-340-0)[\[340\]](#refnote-340) Michael Lee Jr. and [Michael C. Monuteaux](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_Monuteaux "Michael Monuteaux") at Boston Children's Hospital analyzed national emergency department visits among children aged 0 to 17 from 2009 to 2016 using the American Community Survey (ACS) and Nationwide Emergency Department Sample (NEDS). They found no immediate change in pediatric emergency department visit rates the year after the ACA took full effect in 2014, but the rate of change from 2014 to 2016 was significantly higher than previous rate trends, almost 10%.[&#8202;](#ref-back-341-0)[\[341\]](#refnote-341)

Several large insurers formed ACOs. Many hospitals merged and purchased physician practices, amounting to a significant consolidation of the provider industry. The increased market share gave them more leverage with insurers and reduced patient care options.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:15-2)[\[111\]](#refnote-:15)

### Economic consequences

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/11/Obamacare_Panel_2.png/1280px-Obamacare_Panel_2.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Obamacare_Panel_2.png) Coverage rate, employer market cost trends, budgetary impact, and income inequality aspects of the Affordable Care Act

CBO estimated in June 2015 that repealing ACA would:

- Decrease GDP in the short-term, as government spending (on subsidies) was only partially replaced by spending by recipients.
- Increase the supply of labor and aggregate compensation by about 0.8 and 0.9 percent over the 2021–2025 period. CBO cited ACA's expanded eligibility for Medicaid and subsidies and tax credits that rise with income as disincentives to work, so repealing ACA would remove those disincentives, encouraging workers to supply more labor, increasing the total number of hours worked by about 1.5% over the 2021–2025 period.
- Remove the higher tax rates on capital income, thereby encouraging investment, raising the capital stock and output in the long-run.[&#8202;](#ref-back-CBO50252-3)[\[8\]](#refnote-CBO50252)

In 2015 the progressive [Center for Economic and Policy Research](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Center_for_Economic_and_Policy_Research "Center for Economic and Policy Research") found no evidence that companies were reducing worker hours to avoid ACA requirements[&#8202;](#ref-back-342-0)[\[342\]](#refnote-342) for employees working more than 30 hours per week.[&#8202;](#ref-back-343-0)[\[343\]](#refnote-343)

CBO estimated that ACA would slightly reduce the size of the labor force and number of hours worked, as some would no longer be tethered to employers for their insurance. Jonathan Cohn claimed that ACA's primary employment effect was to alleviate [job lock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Job_lock "Job lock")[&#8202;](#ref-back-CBOjobs-0)[\[344\]](#refnote-CBOjobs) and the reform's only significant employment impact was the retirement of those who were working only to stay insured.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ACAEconomyCohn-0)[\[345\]](#refnote-ACAEconomyCohn)

### Abrogated Supreme Court decision

ACA updated a portion of the [False Claims Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/False_Claims_Act_of_1863 "False Claims Act of 1863") that the [United States Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Supreme_Court "United States Supreme Court") interpreted in _[Graham County Soil & Water Conservation District v. United States ex rel. Wilson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Graham_County_Soil_&_Water_Conservation_District_v._United_States_ex_rel._Wilson "Graham County Soil & Water Conservation District v. United States ex rel. Wilson")_ (2010). There, the court said that a subsection giving public officials immunity to _[qui tam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qui_tam "Qui tam")_ lawsuits for false statements in "administrative" disclosures encompassed state officials as well as federal ones. Because of ACA, that section specifies that the immunity is limited to federal disclosures.[&#8202;](#ref-back-346-0)[\[346\]](#refnote-346)

## Public opinion

[![Congressional Democrats celebrating the 6th anniversary of the Affordable Care Act in March 2016 on the steps of the U.S. Capitol.](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a1/6th_anniversary_of_the_Affordable_Care_Act.jpg/500px-6th_anniversary_of_the_Affordable_Care_Act.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:6th_anniversary_of_the_Affordable_Care_Act.jpg) Congressional Democrats celebrate the 6th anniversary of the Affordable Care Act on the steps of the Capitol.

Public views became increasingly negative in reaction to specific plans discussed during the legislative debate over 2009 and 2010. Approval varied by party, race and age. Some elements were more widely favored (preexisting conditions) or opposed (individual mandate).

In a 2010 [poll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Opinion_poll "Opinion poll"), 62% of respondents said they thought ACA would "increase the amount of money they personally spend on health care", 56% said the bill "gives the government too much involvement in health care", and 19% said they thought they and their families would be better off with the legislation.[&#8202;](#ref-back-347-0)[\[347\]](#refnote-347) Other polls found that people were concerned the law would cost more than projected and would not do enough to control costs.[&#8202;](#ref-back-348-0)[\[348\]](#refnote-348)

In a 2012 poll 44% supported the law, with 56% against. By 75% of Democrats, 27% of Independents and 14% of Republicans favored the law. 82% favored banning insurance companies from denying coverage to people with preexisting conditions, 61% favored allowing children to stay on their parents' insurance until age 26, 72% supported requiring companies with more than 50 employees to provide insurance for their employees, and 39% supported the individual mandate to own insurance or pay a penalty. By party affiliation, 19% of Republicans, 27% of Independents, and 59% of Democrats favored the mandate.[&#8202;](#ref-back-most-0)[\[349\]](#refnote-most) Other polls showed additional provisions receiving majority support, including the exchanges, pooling small businesses and the uninsured with other consumers and providing subsidies.[&#8202;](#ref-back-350-0)[\[350\]](#refnote-350)[&#8202;](#ref-back-351-0)[\[351\]](#refnote-351)

Some opponents believed the reform did not go far enough: a 2012 poll indicated that 71% of Republican opponents rejected it overall, while 29% believed it did not go far enough; independent opponents were divided 67% to 33%; and among the much smaller group of Democratic opponents, 49% rejected it overall and 51% wanted more.[&#8202;](#ref-back-most-1)[\[349\]](#refnote-most)

In June 2013, a majority of the public (52–34%) indicated a desire for "Congress to implement or tinker with the law rather than repeal it".[&#8202;](#ref-back-352-0)[\[352\]](#refnote-352) After the Supreme Court upheld the individual mandate, a 2012 poll held that "most Americans (56%) want to see critics of President Obama's health care law drop efforts to block it and move on to other national issues".[&#8202;](#ref-back-353-0)[\[353\]](#refnote-353)

As of October 2013, approximately 40% were in favor while 51% were against.[&#8202;](#ref-back-354-0)[\[354\]](#refnote-354)[&#8202;](#ref-back-355-0)[\[355\]](#refnote-355) About 29% of [whites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-Hispanic_whites "Non-Hispanic whites") approved of the law, compared with 61% of [Hispanics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hispanic_and_Latino_Americans "Hispanic and Latino Americans") and 91% of [African Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_American "African American").[&#8202;](#ref-back-356-0)[\[356\]](#refnote-356) A solid majority of seniors opposed the idea and a solid majority of those under forty were in favor.[&#8202;](#ref-back-357-0)[\[357\]](#refnote-357)

A 2014 poll reported that 26% of Americans support ACA.[&#8202;](#ref-back-358-0)[\[358\]](#refnote-358) A later 2014 poll reported that 48.9% of respondents had an unfavorable view of ACA versus 38.3% who had a favorable view (of more than 5,500 individuals).[&#8202;](#ref-back-359-0)[\[359\]](#refnote-359) Another held that 8% of respondents agreed the Affordable Care Act "is working well the way it is".[&#8202;](#ref-back-360-0)[\[360\]](#refnote-360) In late 2014, a [Rasmussen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rasmussen_Reports "Rasmussen Reports") poll reported Repeal: 30%, Leave as is: 13%, Improve: 52%.[&#8202;](#ref-back-361-0)[\[361\]](#refnote-361)

In 2015, a poll reported that 47% of Americans approved the health care law. This was the first time a major poll indicated that more respondents approved than disapproved.[&#8202;](#ref-back-362-0)[\[362\]](#refnote-362) A December 2016 poll reported that: a) 30% wanted to expand what the law does; b) 26% wanted to repeal the entire law; c) 19% wanted to move forward with implementing the law as it is; and d) 17% wanted to scale back what the law does, with the remainder undecided.[&#8202;](#ref-back-363-0)[\[363\]](#refnote-363)

Separate polls from Fox News and NBC/ _WSJ_, both taken during January 2017, indicated more people viewed the law favorably than did not for the first time. One of the reasons for the improving popularity of the law is that Democrats who had once opposed it (many still prefer "Medicare for all") shifted their positions because ACA was under threat of repeal.[&#8202;](#ref-back-364-0)[\[364\]](#refnote-364) Another January 2017 poll reported that 35% of respondents believed "Obamacare" and the "Affordable Care Act" were different or did not know. (About 45% were unsure whether "repeal of Obamacare" also meant "repeal of the Affordable Care Act".) 39% did not know that "many people would lose coverage through Medicaid or subsidies for private health insurance if the ACA were repealed and no replacement enacted", with Democrats far more likely (79%) to know that fact than Republicans (47%).[&#8202;](#ref-back-365-0)[\[365\]](#refnote-365) A 2017 study found that personal experience with public health insurance programs led to greater support for the ACA, most prominently among Republicans and low-information voters.[&#8202;](#ref-back-366-0)[\[366\]](#refnote-366)

By the end of 2023, a Morning Consult poll of registered voters found that 57% approved of the Affordable Care Act, while 30% disapproved of it. 85% of Democrats, 56% of independents, and 28% of Republicans supported the law.[&#8202;](#ref-back-367-0)[\[367\]](#refnote-367)

## Political aspects

### "Obamacare"

The term "Obamacare" was originally coined by opponents as a [pejorative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pejorative "Pejorative"). According to research by [Elspeth Reeve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elspeth_Reeve "Elspeth Reeve"), the expression was used in early 2007, generally by writers describing the candidate's proposal for expanding coverage for the uninsured.[&#8202;](#ref-back-NYT20120803-0)[\[368\]](#refnote-NYT20120803) The term officially emerged in March 2007 when healthcare [lobbyist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lobbyist "Lobbyist") Jeanne Schulte Scott wrote, "We will soon see a ' [Giuliani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rudy_Giuliani "Rudy Giuliani")-care' and 'Obama-care' to go along with ' [McCain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_McCain "John McCain")-care', ' [Edwards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Edwards "John Edwards")-care', and a totally revamped and remodeled ' [Hillary-care](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hillarycare "Hillarycare")' from the 1990s".[&#8202;](#ref-back-CNN20120625Obamacare-0)[\[369\]](#refnote-CNN20120625Obamacare)[&#8202;](#ref-back-NYT20120325-0)[\[370\]](#refnote-NYT20120325)

In May 2007, [Mitt Romney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mitt_Romney "Mitt Romney") introduced it to political discourse, saying, "How can we get those people insured without raising taxes and without having government take over healthcare?' And let me tell you, if we don't do it, the Democrats will. If the Democrats do it, it will be socialized medicine; it'll be government-managed care. It'll be what's known as Hillarycare or Barack Obamacare, or whatever you want to call it."[&#8202;](#ref-back-CNN20120625Obamacare-1)[\[369\]](#refnote-CNN20120625Obamacare)

By mid-2012, Obamacare had become the [colloquial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Colloquial "Colloquial") term used both by supporters and opponents.[&#8202;](#ref-back-NYT20120803-1)[\[368\]](#refnote-NYT20120803) Obama eventually endorsed the nickname, saying, "I have no problem with people saying Obama cares. I do care."[&#8202;](#ref-back-371-0)[\[371\]](#refnote-371) By 2016 Obama explicitly and repeatedly referred to the ACA as "Obamacare".[&#8202;](#ref-back-obama20160220-0)[\[372\]](#refnote-obama20160220)

The use of "Obamacare" became increasingly rare, and at the [2024 Democratic National Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_Democratic_National_Convention "2024 Democratic National Convention"), Obama said, "I noticed, by the way, since it became popular, they don't call it 'Obamacare' no more."[&#8202;](#ref-back-373-0)[\[373\]](#refnote-373)

### Common misconceptions

#### "Death panels"

Main article: [Death panel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_panel "Death panel")

On August 7, 2009, [Sarah Palin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarah_Palin "Sarah Palin") created the term " [death panels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_panel "Death panel")" to describe groups who would decide whether sick patients were "worthy" of medical care.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Not_so-0)[\[374\]](#refnote-Not_so) "Death panel" referred to two claims about early drafts.

One was that under the law, seniors could be denied care due to their age[&#8202;](#ref-back-375-0)[\[375\]](#refnote-375) and the other that the government would advise seniors to end their lives instead of receiving care. The ostensible basis of these claims was the provision for an [Independent Payment Advisory Board](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Independent_Payment_Advisory_Board "Independent Payment Advisory Board") (IPAB).[&#8202;](#ref-back-salon08132013-0)[\[376\]](#refnote-salon08132013) IPAB was given the authority to recommend cost-saving changes to Medicare by facilitating the adoption of cost-effective treatments and cost-recovering measures when statutory expenditure levels were exceeded within any given three-year period. In fact, the Board was prohibited from recommending changes that would reduce payments before 2020, and was prohibited from recommending changes in premiums, benefits, eligibility and taxes, or other changes that would result in rationing.[&#8202;](#ref-back-377-0)[\[377\]](#refnote-377)[&#8202;](#ref-back-378-0)[\[378\]](#refnote-378)

The other related issue concerned [advance-care planning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Advance_health_care_directive "Advance health care directive") consultation: [a section of the House reform proposal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HR_3200#Reimbursement_for_counseling_about_living_wills "HR 3200") would have reimbursed physicians for providing patient-requested consultations for Medicare recipients on end-of-life health planning (which is covered by many private plans), enabling patients to specify, on request, the kind of care they wished to receive.[&#8202;](#ref-back-379-0)[\[379\]](#refnote-379) The provision was not included in ACA.[&#8202;](#ref-back-380-0)[\[380\]](#refnote-380)

In 2010, the [Pew Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center "Pew Research Center") reported that 85% of Americans were familiar with the claim, and 30% believed it was true, backed by three contemporaneous polls.[&#8202;](#ref-back-381-0)[\[381\]](#refnote-381) The allegation was named [PolitiFact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PolitiFact "PolitiFact")'s 2009 "Lie of the Year",[&#8202;](#ref-back-Not_so-1)[\[374\]](#refnote-Not_so)[&#8202;](#ref-back-382-0)[\[382\]](#refnote-382) one of [FactCheck.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FactCheck.org "FactCheck.org")'s "whoppers"[&#8202;](#ref-back-383-0)[\[383\]](#refnote-383)[&#8202;](#ref-back-384-0)[\[384\]](#refnote-384) and the most outrageous term by the [American Dialect Society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Dialect_Society "American Dialect Society").[&#8202;](#ref-back-385-0)[\[385\]](#refnote-385) [AARP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AARP "AARP") described such rumors as "rife with gross—and even cruel—distortions".[&#8202;](#ref-back-snopes1-0)[\[386\]](#refnote-snopes1)

#### Members of Congress

ACA requires members of Congress and their staffs to obtain health insurance either through an exchange or some other program approved by the law (such as Medicare), instead of using the insurance offered to federal employees (the [Federal Employees Health Benefits Program](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Employees_Health_Benefits_Program "Federal Employees Health Benefits Program")).[&#8202;](#ref-back-387-0)[\[387\]](#refnote-387)[&#8202;](#ref-back-388-0)[\[388\]](#refnote-388)

#### Undocumented immigrants

ACA explicitly denies insurance subsidies to "unauthorized (illegal) [aliens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alien_(law) "Alien (law)")".[&#8202;](#ref-back-cbouninsured-1)[\[53\]](#refnote-cbouninsured)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Chaikind2011-1)[\[54\]](#refnote-Chaikind2011)[&#8202;](#ref-back-389-0)[\[389\]](#refnote-389)

#### Exchange "death spiral"

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d1/County_By_County_Projected_Insurer_Participation_in_Health_Insurance_Exchanges.png/960px-County_By_County_Projected_Insurer_Participation_in_Health_Insurance_Exchanges.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:County_By_County_Projected_Insurer_Participation_in_Health_Insurance_Exchanges.png)

Opponents claimed that combining immediate coverage with no provision for preexisting conditions would lead people to wait to get insured until they got sick. The individual mandate was designed to push people to get insured without waiting. This has been called a "death spiral".[&#8202;](#ref-back-390-0)[\[390\]](#refnote-390) In the years after 2013, many insurers did leave specific marketplaces, claiming the risk pools were too small.

The [median](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Median "Median") number of insurers per state was 4.0 in 2014, 5.0 in 2015, 4.0 in 2016 and 3.0 in 2017. Five states had one insurer in 2017, 13 had two, 11 had three; the remainder had four or more.[&#8202;](#ref-back-391-0)[\[391\]](#refnote-391)

#### "If you like your plan"

At various times during and after ACA debate Obama said, "If you like your health care plan, you'll be able to keep your health care plan."[&#8202;](#ref-back-392-0)[\[392\]](#refnote-392)[&#8202;](#ref-back-ObamaPromise-0)[\[393\]](#refnote-ObamaPromise) However, in fall 2013 millions of Americans with individual policies received notices that their insurance plans were terminated,[&#8202;](#ref-back-394-0)[\[394\]](#refnote-394) and several million more risked seeing their current plans canceled.[&#8202;](#ref-back-395-0)[\[395\]](#refnote-395)[&#8202;](#ref-back-396-0)[\[396\]](#refnote-396)

Obama's previous unambiguous assurance that consumers could keep their own plans became a focal point for critics, who challenged his truthfulness.[&#8202;](#ref-back-397-0)[\[397\]](#refnote-397)[&#8202;](#ref-back-398-0)[\[398\]](#refnote-398) Various bills were introduced in Congress to allow people to keep their plans.[&#8202;](#ref-back-399-0)[\[399\]](#refnote-399)

[PolitiFact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PolitiFact "PolitiFact") initially cited various estimates that only about 2% of the total insured population (4million out of 262 million) received such notices,[&#8202;](#ref-back-400-0)[\[400\]](#refnote-400) but readers later voted Obama's claims as the 2013 "Lie of the Year".[&#8202;](#ref-back-401-0)[\[401\]](#refnote-401)

## Criticism and opposition

Opposition and efforts to repeal the legislation have drawn support from sources that include labor unions,[&#8202;](#ref-back-WSJ-1)[\[338\]](#refnote-WSJ)[&#8202;](#ref-back-wapo-0)[\[402\]](#refnote-wapo) [conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservatism_in_the_United_States "Conservatism in the United States") advocacy groups,[&#8202;](#ref-back-NYT-0)[\[403\]](#refnote-NYT)[&#8202;](#ref-back-NYT-1)[\[404\]](#refnote-NYT) Republicans, small business organizations and the [Tea Party movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tea_Party_movement "Tea Party movement").[&#8202;](#ref-back-405-0)[\[405\]](#refnote-405) These groups claimed the law would disrupt existing health plans, increase costs from new insurance standards, and increase the deficit.[&#8202;](#ref-back-406-0)[\[406\]](#refnote-406) Some opposed the idea of [universal healthcare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_healthcare "Universal healthcare").[&#8202;](#ref-back-407-0)[\[407\]](#refnote-407)[&#8202;](#ref-back-408-0)[\[408\]](#refnote-408) President [Donald Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Trump "Donald Trump") repeatedly promised to "repeal and replace" it.[&#8202;](#ref-back-409-0)[\[409\]](#refnote-409)[&#8202;](#ref-back-410-0)[\[410\]](#refnote-410)

As of 2013[\[update\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Affordable_Care_Act&action=edit) unions that expressed concerns included the [AFL–CIO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AFL%E2%80%93CIO "AFL–CIO"),[&#8202;](#ref-back-AFLCIO-0)[\[411\]](#refnote-AFLCIO) which called ACA "highly disruptive", claiming it would drive up costs of union-sponsored plans; the [International Brotherhood of Teamsters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Brotherhood_of_Teamsters "International Brotherhood of Teamsters"), [United Food and Commercial Workers International Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Food_and_Commercial_Workers_International_Union "United Food and Commercial Workers International Union"), and [UNITE-HERE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UNITE-HERE "UNITE-HERE"), whose leaders argued "PPACA will shatter not only our hard-earned health benefits, but destroy the foundation of the 40-hour work week that is the backbone of the American middle class."[&#8202;](#ref-back-WSJ-2)[\[338\]](#refnote-WSJ) In 2014, [Laborers' International Union of North America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laborers'_International_Union_of_North_America "Laborers' International Union of North America") (LIUNA) president Terry O'Sullivan and [Unite Here](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UNITE_HERE "UNITE HERE") president [D. Taylor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D._Taylor "D. Taylor") wrote, "ACA, as implemented, undermines fair marketplace competition in the health care industry."[&#8202;](#ref-back-wapo-1)[\[402\]](#refnote-wapo)
