In October 2016, [Mark Dayton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Dayton "Mark Dayton"), the governor of Minnesota, said ACA had "many good features" but it was "no longer affordable for increasing numbers of people"; he called on the state legislature to provide emergency relief to policyholders.[&#8202;](#ref-back-412-0)[\[412\]](#refnote-412) Dayton later said he regretted his remarks after they were seized on by Republicans seeking to repeal the law.[&#8202;](#ref-back-413-0)[\[413\]](#refnote-413)

### Socialism debate

Many economically conservative opponents called the ACA " [socialist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialist "Socialist")" or " [socialized medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialized_medicine "Socialized medicine")", pointing to the government redistribution of wealth via subsidies for low-income purchasers of private insurance, expansion of Medicaid, government requirements as to what products can be sold on the exchanges, and the individual mandate, which reduces freedom of consumer choice to be uninsured.[&#8202;](#ref-back-414-0)[\[414\]](#refnote-414)[&#8202;](#ref-back-415-0)[\[415\]](#refnote-415)[&#8202;](#ref-back-416-0)[\[416\]](#refnote-416)

Other observers considered the law a relatively capitalist or "regulated free-market" means of paying for near-universal health care, because it creates new marketplaces with choices for consumers, largely relies on private employers and private health insurance companies, maintains private ownership of hospitals and doctor's offices, and was originally advocated by economic conservatives as a capitalist alternative to [single-payer health care](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Single-payer_health_care "Single-payer health care").[&#8202;](#ref-back-417-0)[\[417\]](#refnote-417)[&#8202;](#ref-back-no_sense-0)[\[418\]](#refnote-no_sense)[&#8202;](#ref-back-obamacarefacts-0)[\[419\]](#refnote-obamacarefacts) Some pointed out that the previous system also had socialist aspects. Even for-profit private health insurance companies socialize risk and redistribute wealth from people who have it (all premium payers) to those who need it (by paying for medically necessary healthcare).[&#8202;](#ref-back-obamacarefacts-1)[\[419\]](#refnote-obamacarefacts) The requirement to provide emergency care also forced redistribution from people who pay insurance premiums to those who choose to be uninsured, when they visit the emergency room.[&#8202;](#ref-back-no_sense-1)[\[418\]](#refnote-no_sense)

Some ACA supporters accused conservatives of using the term "socialism" as a negative buzzword for ACA as it was for Medicare and Medicaid,[&#8202;](#ref-back-obamacarefacts-2)[\[419\]](#refnote-obamacarefacts) and some embraced the label "socialism" as desirable, distinguishing [democratic socialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_socialism "Democratic socialism") as desirable for education and health care[&#8202;](#ref-back-420-0)[\[420\]](#refnote-420) and [communism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communism "Communism") as undesirable.[&#8202;](#ref-back-obamacarefacts-3)[\[419\]](#refnote-obamacarefacts) Milos Forman opined that critics "falsely equate Western European-style socialism, and its government provision of social insurance and health care, with Marxist–Leninist totalitarianism".[&#8202;](#ref-back-421-0)[\[421\]](#refnote-421)

### Legal challenges

Main article: [Constitutional challenges to the Patient Protection and Affordable Care Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_challenges_to_the_Patient_Protection_and_Affordable_Care_Act "Constitutional challenges to the Patient Protection and Affordable Care Act")

See also: [National Federation of Independent Business v. Sebelius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Federation_of_Independent_Business_v._Sebelius "National Federation of Independent Business v. Sebelius"), [King v. Burwell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_v._Burwell "King v. Burwell"), and [Efforts to repeal the Patient Protection and Affordable Care Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Efforts_to_repeal_the_Patient_Protection_and_Affordable_Care_Act "Efforts to repeal the Patient Protection and Affordable Care Act")

#### _National Federation of Independent Business v. Sebelius_

Main article: [National Federation of Independent Business v. Sebelius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Federation_of_Independent_Business_v._Sebelius "National Federation of Independent Business v. Sebelius")

Opponents challenged ACA's constitutionality in multiple lawsuits on multiple grounds.[&#8202;](#ref-back-422-0)[\[422\]](#refnote-422)[&#8202;](#ref-back-423-0)[\[423\]](#refnote-423)\[ _[failed verification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability "Wikipedia:Verifiability")_\] The Supreme Court ruled, 5–4, that the individual mandate was constitutional when viewed as a tax, although [not](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitutional_challenges_to_the_Patient_Protection_and_Affordable_Care_Act#U.S._Supreme_Court "Constitutional challenges to the Patient Protection and Affordable Care Act") under the [Commerce Clause](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commerce_Clause "Commerce Clause").

ACA withheld all Medicaid funding from states declining to participate in the expansion. The Court ruled that this was unconstitutionally coercive and that individual states had the right to opt out without losing preexisting Medicaid funding.[&#8202;](#ref-back-NatLawReview2012-1)[\[13\]](#refnote-NatLawReview2012)

#### Contraception mandate

Main articles: [Burwell v. Hobby Lobby Stores, Inc.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burwell_v._Hobby_Lobby_Stores,_Inc. "Burwell v. Hobby Lobby Stores, Inc.") and [Little Sisters of the Poor Saints Peter and Paul Home v. Pennsylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Little_Sisters_of_the_Poor_Saints_Peter_and_Paul_Home_v._Pennsylvania "Little Sisters of the Poor Saints Peter and Paul Home v. Pennsylvania")

In March 2012, the [Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church "Catholic Church"), while supportive of ACA's objectives, voiced concern through the [United States Conference of Catholic Bishops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USCCB "USCCB") that aspects of the mandate covering contraception and sterilization and [HHS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Department_of_Health_and_Human_Services "United States Department of Health and Human Services")'s narrow definition of a religious organization violated the [First Amendment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Amendment_to_the_United_States_Constitution "First Amendment to the United States Constitution") right to [free exercise of religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_Exercise_Clause "Free Exercise Clause") and conscience. Various lawsuits addressed these concerns,[&#8202;](#ref-back-424-0)[\[424\]](#refnote-424)[&#8202;](#ref-back-425-0)[\[425\]](#refnote-425) including _[Burwell v. Hobby Lobby Stores, Inc.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burwell_v._Hobby_Lobby_Stores,_Inc. "Burwell v. Hobby Lobby Stores, Inc.")_, which looked at private corporations and their duties under the ACA.

In _[Little Sisters of the Poor Saints Peter and Paul Home v. Pennsylvania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Little_Sisters_of_the_Poor_Saints_Peter_and_Paul_Home_v._Pennsylvania "Little Sisters of the Poor Saints Peter and Paul Home v. Pennsylvania")_, the Supreme Court ruled 7–2 on July 8, 2020, that employers with religious or moral objections to contraceptives can exclude such coverage from an employee's insurance plan. Writing for the majority, Justice [Clarence Thomas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clarence_Thomas "Clarence Thomas") said, "No language in the statute itself even hints that Congress intended that contraception should or must be covered. It was Congress, not the \[administration\], that declined to expressly require contraceptive coverage in the ACA itself." Justices Roberts, Alito, Gorsuch, and Kavanaugh joined Thomas's opinion. Justice [Elena Kagan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elena_Kagan "Elena Kagan") filed a concurring opinion in the judgment, in which [Stephen Breyer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stephen_Breyer "Stephen Breyer") joined. Justices Ginsburg and Sotomayor dissented, saying the court's ruling "leaves women workers to fend for themselves."[&#8202;](#ref-back-426-0)[\[426\]](#refnote-426)

In a later lawsuit brought by private health insurance buyers and businesses, Judge [Reed O'Connor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reed_O'Connor "Reed O'Connor") of the Federal District Court for the Northern District of Texas ruled in March 2023 that the ACA's provision of contraceptives, HIV testing, and screenings for cancer, diabetes, and mental health violated the plaintiffs' freedom of religious exercise, and placed an injunction on that portion of the ACA. The Biden administration planned to seek a hold on O'Connor's decision.[&#8202;](#ref-back-427-0)[\[427\]](#refnote-427)

#### _King v Burwell_

Main article: [King v. Burwell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_v._Burwell "King v. Burwell")

On June 25, 2015, the U.S. Supreme Court ruled, 6–3, that federal subsidies for health insurance premiums could be used in the 34 states that did not set up their own insurance exchanges.[&#8202;](#ref-back-428-0)[\[428\]](#refnote-428)

#### _House v. Price_

Main article: [House v. Price](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_v._Price "House v. Price")

House Republicans sued the Obama administration in 2014, alleging that cost-sharing reduction subsidy payments to insurers were unlawful because Congress had not appropriated funds to pay for them. The argument classified the CSR subsidy as discretionary spending subject to annual appropriation. In May 2016, a federal judge ruled for the plaintiffs, but the Obama administration appealed.[&#8202;](#ref-back-429-0)[\[429\]](#refnote-429) Later, President Trump ended the payments. This led to further litigation.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:3-0)[\[430\]](#refnote-:3)

#### _United States House of Representatives v. Azar_

Main article: [United States House of Representatives v. Azar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_House_of_Representatives_v._Azar "United States House of Representatives v. Azar")

The House sued the administration, alleging that the money for CSRs to insurers had not been appropriated, as required for any federal government spending. The ACA subsidy that helps customers pay premiums was not part of the suit.

Without the CSRs, the government estimated that premiums would increase by 20% to 30% for silver plans.[&#8202;](#ref-back-431-0)[\[431\]](#refnote-431) In 2017, the uncertainty about whether the payments would continue caused Blue Cross Blue Shield of North Carolina to try to raise premiums by 22.9% the next year, as opposed to an increase of 8.8% that it would have sought if the payments were assured.[&#8202;](#ref-back-432-0)[\[432\]](#refnote-432)

U.S. District Judge [Rosemary M. Collyer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rosemary_M._Collyer "Rosemary M. Collyer") ruled that the cost-sharing program was unconstitutional for spending money that has not been specifically provided by an act of Congress, but concluded that Congress had in fact authorized that program to be created. The judge also found that Congress had provided authority to cover the spending for the tax credits to consumers who use them to help afford health coverage.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Denniston-0)[\[433\]](#refnote-Denniston) Collyer enjoined further cost-sharing payments, but stayed the order pending appeal to the [United States Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Court_of_Appeals_for_the_District_of_Columbia_Circuit "United States Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit"). The case ended in a settlement before the Circuit Court.

#### _California v. Texas_

Main article: [California v. Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/California_v._Texas "California v. Texas")

Texas and 19 other states filed a civil suit in the [United States District Court for the Northern District of Texas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_District_Court_for_the_Northern_District_of_Texas "United States District Court for the Northern District of Texas") in February 2018, arguing that with the passage of the [Tax Cuts and Jobs Act of 2017](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tax_Cuts_and_Jobs_Act_of_2017 "Tax Cuts and Jobs Act of 2017"), which eliminated the tax for not having health insurance, the individual mandate no longer had a constitutional basis and thus the entire ACA was no longer constitutional.[&#8202;](#ref-back-434-0)[\[434\]](#refnote-434) The [Department of Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Department_of_Justice "United States Department of Justice") said it would no longer defend the ACA in court, but 17 states led by California stepped in to do so.[&#8202;](#ref-back-cnn_20181214-0)[\[435\]](#refnote-cnn_20181214)

District Judge [Reed O'Connor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reed_O'Connor "Reed O'Connor") of Texas ruled for the plaintiffs on December 14, 2018, writing that the "Individual Mandate can no longer be fairly read as an exercise of Congress's Tax Power and is still impermissible under the Interstate Commerce Clause—meaning the Individual Mandate is unconstitutional." He then further reasoned that the individual mandate is an essential part of the entire law, and thus was not severable, making the entire law unconstitutional.[&#8202;](#ref-back-436-0)[\[436\]](#refnote-436)[&#8202;](#ref-back-437-0)[\[437\]](#refnote-437) O'Connor's decision regarding severability turned on several passages from the Congressional debate that focused on the importance of the mandate.[&#8202;](#ref-back-438-0)[\[438\]](#refnote-438) While he ruled the law unconstitutional, he did not overturn the law.[&#8202;](#ref-back-cnn_20181214-1)[\[435\]](#refnote-cnn_20181214)

The intervening states appealed the decision to the [Fifth Circuit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Court_of_Appeals_for_the_Fifth_Circuit "United States Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit"). These states argued that Congress's change in the tax was only reducing the amount of the tax, and that Congress had the power to write a stronger law to this end.[&#8202;](#ref-back-439-0)[\[439\]](#refnote-439)[&#8202;](#ref-back-wapost_20181214-0)[\[440\]](#refnote-wapost_20181214) O'Connor stayed his decision pending the appeal.[&#8202;](#ref-back-441-0)[\[441\]](#refnote-441) The Fifth Circuit heard the appeal on July 9, 2019; in the interim, the U.S. Department of Justice joined with Republican states to argue that the ACA was unconstitutional, while the Democratic states were joined by the Democrat-controlled U.S. House of Representatives. An additional question was addressed, as the Republican plaintiffs challenged the Democratic states' [standing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standing_(law) "Standing (law)") to defend the ACA.[&#8202;](#ref-back-442-0)[\[442\]](#refnote-442)

In December 2019, the Fifth Circuit agreed the individual mandate was unconstitutional, but did not agree that the entire law should be voided. Instead, it remanded the case to the District Court for reconsideration of that question.[&#8202;](#ref-back-443-0)[\[443\]](#refnote-443) The Supreme Court accepted the case in March 2020, to be heard in the 2020–2021 term,[&#8202;](#ref-back-444-0)[\[444\]](#refnote-444) with the ruling likely falling after the 2020 elections.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ollstein-0)[\[445\]](#refnote-ollstein)

Democrats pointed out that the effect of invalidating the entire law would be to remove popular provisions such as the protection for preexisting conditions, and that the Republicans had still not offered any replacement plan—important issues in the 2020 elections.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ollstein-1)[\[445\]](#refnote-ollstein)

On June 17, 2021, the Court rejected the challenge in a 7–2 decision, ruling that Texas and the other plaintiff states did not have standing to challenge the provision, leaving the full ACA intact.[&#8202;](#ref-back-446-0)[\[446\]](#refnote-446)[&#8202;](#ref-back-447-0)[\[447\]](#refnote-447)[&#8202;](#ref-back-448-0)[\[448\]](#refnote-448)

#### Section 1557

In April 2024, the Biden administration issued a final rule implementing Section 1557 of the ACA, adding [gender identity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender_identity "Gender identity") to [Title IX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Title_IX "Title IX")'s definition of discrimination based on [sex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biological_sex "Biological sex").[&#8202;](#ref-back-449-0)[\[449\]](#refnote-449)[&#8202;](#ref-back-450-0)[\[450\]](#refnote-450) In May 2024, 15 states led by [Tennessee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tennessee "Tennessee") sued HHS in the [U.S. District Court for the Southern District of Mississippi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_District_Court_for_the_Southern_District_of_Mississippi "United States District Court for the Southern District of Mississippi"), arguing that the 2024 rule violated the [Administrative Procedure Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Administrative_Procedure_Act "Administrative Procedure Act") and the U.S. Constitution.[&#8202;](#ref-back-451-0)[\[451\]](#refnote-451) In October 2025, a federal judge struck down the 2024 rule to the extent that it prohibits discrimination against [transgender](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transgender "Transgender") people in education and health-care programs.[&#8202;](#ref-back-452-0)[\[452\]](#refnote-452)

#### Risk corridors

Further information: [Maine Community Health Options v. United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maine_Community_Health_Options_v._United_States "Maine Community Health Options v. United States")

The Supreme Court ruled that promised risk corridor payments must be made even in the absence of specific appropriation of money by Congress.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:1-1)[\[85\]](#refnote-:1)

#### Non-cooperation

Officials in Texas, Florida, Alabama, Wyoming, Arizona, Oklahoma and Missouri opposed those elements over which they had discretion.[&#8202;](#ref-back-453-0)[\[453\]](#refnote-453)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Ornstein-0)[\[454\]](#refnote-Ornstein) For example, Missouri declined to expand Medicaid or [establish a health insurance marketplace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delay_in_application_of_the_Individual_Health_Insurance_Mandate "Delay in application of the Individual Health Insurance Mandate") engaging in active [non-cooperation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nonviolence#Noncooperation "Nonviolence"), enacting a statute forbidding any state or local official to render any aid not specifically required by federal law.[&#8202;](#ref-back-NYT80213-0)[\[455\]](#refnote-NYT80213) Other Republicans discouraged efforts to advertise the law's benefits. Some conservative political groups launched ad campaigns to discourage enrollment.[&#8202;](#ref-back-456-0)[\[456\]](#refnote-456)[&#8202;](#ref-back-457-0)[\[457\]](#refnote-457)

### Repeal and modification efforts

Main article: [Efforts to repeal the Affordable Care Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Efforts_to_repeal_the_Affordable_Care_Act "Efforts to repeal the Affordable Care Act")

ACA was the subject of many unsuccessful repeal efforts by [Republicans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_Party_(United_States) "Republican Party (United States)") in the [111th](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/111th_United_States_Congress "111th United States Congress"), [112th](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/112th_United_States_Congress "112th United States Congress"), and [113th](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/113th_United_States_Congress "113th United States Congress") Congresses: Representatives [Steve King](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steve_King "Steve King") and [Michele Bachmann](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michele_Bachmann "Michele Bachmann") introduced bills in the House to repeal the ACA the day after it was signed, as did Senator [Jim DeMint](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_DeMint "Jim DeMint") in the Senate.[&#8202;](#ref-back-458-0)[\[458\]](#refnote-458) In 2011, after Republicans gained control of the House, one of the first votes held was on a bill titled "Repealing the Job-Killing Health Care Law Act" (H.R. 2), which the House passed 245–189.[&#8202;](#ref-back-459-0)[\[459\]](#refnote-459) All Republicans and three Democrats voted for repeal.[&#8202;](#ref-back-460-0)[\[460\]](#refnote-460) In the Senate, the bill was offered as an amendment to an unrelated bill, but was voted down.[&#8202;](#ref-back-461-0)[\[461\]](#refnote-461) President Obama said he would [veto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Presidential_veto "U.S. Presidential veto") the bill had it passed.[&#8202;](#ref-back-462-0)[\[462\]](#refnote-462)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2c/2017_House_budget.pdf/page1-500px-2017_House_budget.pdf.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:2017_House_budget.pdf) 2017 House Budget

On February 3, 2015, the House of Representatives added its 67th repeal vote to the record (239 to 186). This attempt also failed.[&#8202;](#ref-back-463-0)[\[463\]](#refnote-463)

#### 2013 federal government shutdown

Strong partisan disagreement in Congress prevented adjustments to the Act's provisions.[&#8202;](#ref-back-NYT52613-0)[\[464\]](#refnote-NYT52613) But at least one change, a proposed repeal of a tax on medical devices, received bipartisan support.[&#8202;](#ref-back-465-0)[\[465\]](#refnote-465) Some Congressional Republicans argued against improvements to the law on the grounds that they would weaken the arguments for repeal.[&#8202;](#ref-back-MandateRepeal-3)[\[334\]](#refnote-MandateRepeal)[&#8202;](#ref-back-ChaitNotCollapsing-0)[\[466\]](#refnote-ChaitNotCollapsing)

Republicans attempted to defund the ACA's implementation,[&#8202;](#ref-back-Ornstein-1)[\[454\]](#refnote-Ornstein)[&#8202;](#ref-back-467-0)[\[467\]](#refnote-467) and in October 2013 House Republicans refused to fund the federal government unless it came with an implementation delay, after Obama unilaterally deferred the employer mandate by one year, which critics claimed he had no power to do. The House passed three versions of a bill funding the government while submitting various versions that would repeal or delay the ACA, with the last version delaying enforcement of the individual mandate. The Democratic Senate leadership said the Senate would pass only a bill without any restrictions on ACA. [The government shutdown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_federal_government_shutdown_of_2013 "United States federal government shutdown of 2013") lasted from October 1 to October 17.[&#8202;](#ref-back-468-0)[\[468\]](#refnote-468)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Beutler-0)[\[469\]](#refnote-Beutler)[&#8202;](#ref-back-470-0)[\[470\]](#refnote-470)

#### 2017 repeal effort

Main article: [2017 Patient Protection and Affordable Care Act replacement proposals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Patient_Protection_and_Affordable_Care_Act_replacement_proposals "2017 Patient Protection and Affordable Care Act replacement proposals")

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/44/John_McCain_Votes_No_on_Obamacare_Repeal.jpg/500px-John_McCain_Votes_No_on_Obamacare_Repeal.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:John_McCain_Votes_No_on_Obamacare_Repeal.jpg) McCain votes no on repealing the Affordable Care Act by giving a thumbs down.

During a midnight congressional session starting January 11, the Senate of the [115th Congress of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/115th_United_States_Congress "115th United States Congress") voted to approve a "budget blueprint" that would allow [Republicans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_Party_(United_States) "Republican Party (United States)") to repeal parts of the law "without threat of a [Democratic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Party_(United_States) "Democratic Party (United States)") [filibuster](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filibuster "Filibuster")".[&#8202;](#ref-back-471-0)[\[471\]](#refnote-471)[&#8202;](#ref-back-472-0)[\[472\]](#refnote-472) The plan, which passed 51–48, was named by Senate Republicans the "Obamacare 'repeal resolution.'"[&#8202;](#ref-back-473-0)[\[473\]](#refnote-473) Democrats opposing the resolution staged a protest during the vote.[&#8202;](#ref-back-474-0)[\[474\]](#refnote-474)

[House Republicans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republican_Conference_of_the_United_States_House_of_Representatives "Republican Conference of the United States House of Representatives") announced their replacement, the [American Health Care Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Health_Care_Act "American Health Care Act"), on March 6.[&#8202;](#ref-back-475-0)[\[475\]](#refnote-475) On March 24, the AHCA failed amid a revolt among Republican representatives.[&#8202;](#ref-back-476-0)[\[476\]](#refnote-476)

On May 4 the House voted to pass the AHCA by a margin of 217 to 213.[&#8202;](#ref-back-477-0)[\[477\]](#refnote-477) The Senate Republican leadership announced that Senate Republicans would write their own version of the bill instead of voting on the House version.[&#8202;](#ref-back-478-0)[\[478\]](#refnote-478)

[Leader](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Party_leaders_of_the_United_States_Senate "Party leaders of the United States Senate") McConnell named a group of 13 Republicans to draft the substitute version in private, raising bipartisan concerns about lack of transparency.[&#8202;](#ref-back-479-0)[\[479\]](#refnote-479)[&#8202;](#ref-back-480-0)[\[480\]](#refnote-480)[&#8202;](#ref-back-481-0)[\[481\]](#refnote-481) On June 22, Republicans released the first discussion draft, which renamed it the "Better Care Reconciliation Act of 2017" (BCRA).[&#8202;](#ref-back-482-0)[\[482\]](#refnote-482) On July 25, although no amendment proposal had garnered majority support, Republicans voted to advance the bill to the floor and begin formal consideration of amendments. Senators [Susan Collins](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Susan_Collins "Susan Collins") and [Lisa Murkowski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lisa_Murkowski "Lisa Murkowski") were the only two dissenting Republicans, making the vote a 50–50 tie. Vice President [Mike Pence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mike_Pence "Mike Pence") then cast the tie-breaking vote in the affirmative.[&#8202;](#ref-back-483-0)[\[483\]](#refnote-483)

The revised BCRA failed, 43–57. A subsequent "Obamacare Repeal and Reconciliation Act" abandoned the "repeal and replace" approach in favor of a straight repeal, but that too failed, 45–55. Finally, the "Health Care Freedom Act", nicknamed "skinny repeal" because it would have made the least change to ACA, failed by 49–51, with Collins, Murkowski, and McCain joining all Democrats and independents in voting against it.[&#8202;](#ref-back-484-0)[\[484\]](#refnote-484)

#### Proposed changes in 2024

Donald Trump, who has historically opposed the ACA,[&#8202;](#ref-back-485-0)[\[485\]](#refnote-485)[&#8202;](#ref-back-486-0)[\[486\]](#refnote-486)[&#8202;](#ref-back-487-0)[\[487\]](#refnote-487) said during the [2024 United States presidential debates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2024_United_States_presidential_debates "2024 United States presidential debates") that he had "concepts of a plan" to modify or scrap it. [JD Vance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/JD_Vance "JD Vance") has said that Trump intends to allow insurance companies to discriminate against people with preexisting conditions or [disability](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disability "Disability"), with [subsidized insurance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_safety_net "Social safety net") replaced with private insurance.[&#8202;](#ref-back-488-0)[\[488\]](#refnote-488)[&#8202;](#ref-back-489-0)[\[489\]](#refnote-489)[&#8202;](#ref-back-490-0)[\[490\]](#refnote-490) Kamala Harris said during [her 2024 campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamala_Harris_2024_presidential_campaign "Kamala Harris 2024 presidential campaign") that she would "maintain and grow" the ACA.[&#8202;](#ref-back-491-0)[\[491\]](#refnote-491)[&#8202;](#ref-back-492-0)[\[492\]](#refnote-492)

#### 2025 federal government shutdown

During the [2025 federal government shutdown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_United_States_federal_government_shutdown "2025 United States federal government shutdown"), the ACA once again became a focal point of partisan disagreement. On November 8–9, 2025, as the shutdown reached its 39th day, President [Donald Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Trump "Donald Trump") urged Senate Republicans to redirect federal funds used for ACA insurance subsidies toward direct cash payments to individuals, framing the proposal as a strategy to resolve the budgetary impasse. Several Republican senators expressed openness to the idea; Democrats rejected it. Analysts noted that the proposal lacked the bipartisan support necessary to reopen the federal government. Contemporary reports described the dispute over the ACA's subsidy structure as one of the key issues contributing to the protracted shutdown.[&#8202;](#ref-back-493-0)[\[493\]](#refnote-493) Trump said he believed the law will expire once its tax credit is eliminated and not renewed.[&#8202;](#ref-back-494-0)[\[494\]](#refnote-494)

The disagreement focused on whether to continue the enhanced premium tax credits expanded under previous legislation, including the American Rescue Plan, which lowered the portion of health insurance costs consumers pay. Observers noted that if these subsidies expired, many individuals could face significant premium increases, potentially more than doubling for some enrollees. This raised concerns about decreased enrollment in the ACA marketplaces, especially among individuals who previously received little or no subsidy.[&#8202;](#ref-back-495-0)[\[495\]](#refnote-495)

In addition to the effects on consumers, small businesses and self-employed individuals relying on ACA marketplace plans faced potential financial strain. Reports also highlighted that certain federal health agency employees were furloughed during the shutdown, temporarily affecting the oversight and administration of health programs. The debate over ACA funding illustrated how federal budget impasses can have immediate effects on both insurance markets and the delivery of health services.[&#8202;](#ref-back-496-0)[\[496\]](#refnote-496)[&#8202;](#ref-back-497-0)[\[497\]](#refnote-497)[&#8202;](#ref-back-498-0)[\[498\]](#refnote-498)

### Actions to hinder implementation

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/3/36/2-TCJA_Number_Uninsured_v1.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail_unscaled)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:2-TCJA_Number_Uninsured_v1.png) Tax Cuts and Jobs Act—number of additional persons uninsured[&#8202;](#ref-back-499-0)[\[499\]](#refnote-499)

Under both the ACA (current law) and the AHCA, the CBO reported that the health exchange marketplaces would remain stable.[&#8202;](#ref-back-CBO_Score1-0)[\[500\]](#refnote-CBO_Score1) But Republican politicians took a variety of steps to undermine it, creating uncertainty that adversely impacted enrollment and insurer participation while increasing premiums.[&#8202;](#ref-back-501-0)[\[501\]](#refnote-501) Concern about the exchanges became another argument for reforms. Past and ongoing Republican attempts to weaken the law have included:

- Lawsuits such as _[King v. Burwell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_v._Burwell "King v. Burwell")_ and _[House v. Price](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_v._Price "House v. Price")_.
- President Trump ended the payment of [cost-sharing reduction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cost_sharing_reductions_subsidy "Cost sharing reductions subsidy") subsidies to insurers on October 12, 2017. CBO estimated in September 2017 that discontinuing the payments would add an average of 15–20 percentage points to health insurance costs on the exchanges in 2018 while increasing the budget deficit nearly $200 billion over a decade.[&#8202;](#ref-back-VoxCBO1-0)[\[502\]](#refnote-VoxCBO1) In response, insurers sued the government for reimbursement. Various cases are under appeal as of 2019.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:3-1)[\[430\]](#refnote-:3) Several insurers and actuarial groups estimated this resulted in a 20 percentage point or more increase in premiums for the 2018 plan year. In other words, premium increases expected to be 10% or less in 2018 became 28–40% instead.[&#8202;](#ref-back-503-0)[\[503\]](#refnote-503)[&#8202;](#ref-back-504-0)[\[504\]](#refnote-504) The insurers would need to make up the $7 billion they had previously received in cost-sharing reductions (CSRs) by raising premiums. Since most premiums are subsidized, the federal government would cover most of the increases. CBO also estimated that initially up to one million fewer people would have health insurance coverage, although rising subsidies might eventually offset this. The 85% of enrollees who received subsidies would be unaffected. CBO expected the exchanges to remain stable (i.e., no "death spiral" before or after Trump's action) as the premiums would increase and prices would stabilize at the higher (non-CSR) level.[&#8202;](#ref-back-505-0)[\[505\]](#refnote-505) Several insurance companies who sued the United States for failure to pay CSRs won cases in 2018 and 2019. The judiciary decided the insurance companies are entitled to unpaid CSRs.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:3-2)[\[430\]](#refnote-:3)[&#8202;](#ref-back-506-0)[\[506\]](#refnote-506)
- The 2015 appropriations bill had a rider that ended the payment of risk corridor funds. This was repeated in later years. This resulted in the bankruptcy of many co-ops. This action was attributed to Senator [Marco Rubio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marco_Rubio "Marco Rubio").[&#8202;](#ref-back-507-0)[\[507\]](#refnote-507) The cutoff generated some 50 lawsuits. The Supreme Court granted [certiorari](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Certiorari "Certiorari") in 2019 in the case _[Maine Community Health Options v. United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maine_Community_Health_Options_v._United_States "Maine Community Health Options v. United States")_.[&#8202;](#ref-back-508-0)[\[508\]](#refnote-508)[&#8202;](#ref-back-509-0)[\[509\]](#refnote-509)[&#8202;](#ref-back-510-0)[\[510\]](#refnote-510)
- Trump weakened the individual mandate with his first executive order, which limited enforcement of the tax. For example, tax returns without indications of health insurance ("silent returns") will still be processed, overriding Obama's instructions to reject them.[&#8202;](#ref-back-511-0)[\[511\]](#refnote-511)
- Trump reduced funding for advertising for exchange enrollment by up to 90%, with other reductions to support resources used to answer questions and help people sign-up for coverage.[&#8202;](#ref-back-512-0)[\[512\]](#refnote-512) The CBO said the reductions would reduce ACA enrollment.[&#8202;](#ref-back-VoxCBO1-1)[\[502\]](#refnote-VoxCBO1)
- Trump reduced the enrollment period for 2018 by half, to 45 days.[&#8202;](#ref-back-513-0)[\[513\]](#refnote-513)
- Trump made public statements that the exchanges were unstable or in a [death spiral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_spiral_(insurance) "Death spiral (insurance)").[&#8202;](#ref-back-514-0)[\[514\]](#refnote-514)

### Perceived inadequacies

In December 2009, former DNC chairman and former Vermont governor [Howard Dean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Howard_Dean "Howard Dean") called the ACA "a bigger [bailout](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bailout "Bailout") for the insurance industry than [AIG](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_International_Group#2008_liquidity_crisis_and_government_bailout "American International Group")" and "an insurance company's dream". He viewed the bill's end form as a death of the health care reform effort.[&#8202;](#ref-back-515-0)[\[515\]](#refnote-515)

In his 2011 book _Remedy and Reaction_, [Paul Starr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Starr "Paul Starr"), the former senior advisor for [Bill Clinton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_Clinton "Bill Clinton")'s [health care reform plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clinton_health_care_plan_of_1993 "Clinton health care plan of 1993"), notes that the ACA did not make [health insurance a right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right_to_health "Right to health") and did not make medical care free at the [point of service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Point_of_sale "Point of sale"). He criticizies the ACA on the grounds that some lower-income individuals still cannot afford treatment and go without care "if health care and insurance are treated as ordinary commodities".[&#8202;](#ref-back-516-0)[\[516\]](#refnote-516)

The ACA's critics often cite its inability to control costs and lower deductibles,[&#8202;](#ref-back-517-0)[\[517\]](#refnote-517)[&#8202;](#ref-back-518-0)[\[518\]](#refnote-518) the difficulty for average people to compare plans,[&#8202;](#ref-back-519-0)[\[519\]](#refnote-519) lack of a strong public option, and inadequate regulations on or alternatives (such as [co-operatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cooperative "Cooperative")) to large corporate health care companies.[&#8202;](#ref-back-520-0)[\[520\]](#refnote-520)

## Implementation

Main article: [Implementation history of the Patient Protection and Affordable Care Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Implementation_history_of_the_Patient_Protection_and_Affordable_Care_Act "Implementation history of the Patient Protection and Affordable Care Act")

In 2010 small business tax credits took effect.[&#8202;](#ref-back-hist-0)[\[521\]](#refnote-hist) Then [Pre-Existing Condition Insurance Plan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pre-existing_Condition_Insurance_Plan "Pre-existing Condition Insurance Plan") (PCIP) took effect to offer insurance to those who had been denied coverage by private insurance companies because of a preexisting condition.[&#8202;](#ref-back-hist-1)[\[521\]](#refnote-hist) By 2011, insurers had stopped marketing child-only policies in 17 states, as they sought to escape this requirement.[&#8202;](#ref-back-522-0)[\[522\]](#refnote-522) In _[National Federation of Independent Business v. Sebelius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Federation_of_Independent_Business_v._Sebelius "National Federation of Independent Business v. Sebelius")_ the Supreme Court allowed states to opt out of the Medicaid expansion.[&#8202;](#ref-back-523-0)[\[523\]](#refnote-523)[&#8202;](#ref-back-524-0)[\[524\]](#refnote-524)[&#8202;](#ref-back-525-0)[\[525\]](#refnote-525)

In 2013, the [Internal Revenue Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internal_Revenue_Service "Internal Revenue Service") ruled that the cost of covering only the individual employee would be considered in determining whether the cost of coverage exceeded 9.5% of income. Family plans would not be considered even if the cost was above the 9.5% income threshold.[&#8202;](#ref-back-526-0)[\[526\]](#refnote-526)[&#8202;](#ref-back-527-0)[\[527\]](#refnote-527) On July2 Obama delayed the employer mandate until 2015.[&#8202;](#ref-back-CohnDelay-2)[\[332\]](#refnote-CohnDelay)[&#8202;](#ref-back-treasurystatement-0)[\[528\]](#refnote-treasurystatement)[&#8202;](#ref-back-REG-0)[\[529\]](#refnote-REG-138006) The launch for both the state and federal exchanges was beset by management and technical failings. [HealthCare.gov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HealthCare.gov "HealthCare.gov"), the website that offers insurance through the exchanges operated by the federal government, crashed on opening and suffered many problems.[&#8202;](#ref-back-530-0)[\[530\]](#refnote-530) Operations stabilized in 2014, although not all planned features were complete.[&#8202;](#ref-back-531-0)[\[531\]](#refnote-531)[&#8202;](#ref-back-532-0)[\[532\]](#refnote-532)

The [Government Accountability Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_Accountability_Office "Government Accountability Office") released a non-partisan study in 2014 that concluded the administration had not provided "effective planning or oversight practices" in developing the exchanges.[&#8202;](#ref-back-AP-0)[\[533\]](#refnote-AP) In _[Burwell v. Hobby Lobby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burwell_v._Hobby_Lobby "Burwell v. Hobby Lobby")_ the Supreme Court exempted closely held corporations with religious convictions from the contraception rule.[&#8202;](#ref-back-:2-0)[\[534\]](#refnote-:2) At the beginning of the 2015, 11.7 million had signed up (ex-Medicaid).[&#8202;](#ref-back-535-0)[\[535\]](#refnote-535) By the end of the year about 8.8 million consumers had stayed in the program.[&#8202;](#ref-back-536-0)[\[536\]](#refnote-536) Congress repeatedly delayed the onset of the " [Cadillac tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cadillac_tax "Cadillac tax")" on expensive insurance plans first until 2020[&#8202;](#ref-back-537-0)[\[537\]](#refnote-537) and later until 2022 and repealed it in late 2019.[&#8202;](#ref-back-auto1-2)[\[98\]](#refnote-auto1)

An estimated 9 to 10 million people had gained Medicaid coverage in 2016, mostly low-income adults. The five major national insurers expected to lose money on ACA policies in 2016,[&#8202;](#ref-back-538-0)[\[538\]](#refnote-538) in part because the enrollees were lower income, older and sicker than expected.[&#8202;](#ref-back-539-0)[\[539\]](#refnote-539)

More than 9.2 million people (3.0 million new customers and 6.2 million returning) enrolled on the national exchange in 2017, down some 400,000 from 2016. This decline was due primarily to the election of President Trump.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ACA_NYT2017-0)[\[540\]](#refnote-ACA_NYT2017) The eleven states that run their own exchanges signed up about 3million more.[&#8202;](#ref-back-ACA_NYT2017-1)[\[540\]](#refnote-ACA_NYT2017) The IRS announced that it would not require that tax returns indicate a person has health insurance, reducing the effectiveness of the individual mandate, in response to Trump's executive order.[&#8202;](#ref-back-541-0)[\[541\]](#refnote-541) The CBO reported in March that the healthcare exchanges were expected to be stable.[&#8202;](#ref-back-CBO_Score1-1)[\[500\]](#refnote-CBO_Score1) In May the House voted to repeal the ACA using the American Health Care Act (AHCA), but the AHCA was defeated in the Senate.[&#8202;](#ref-back-542-0)[\[542\]](#refnote-542)[&#8202;](#ref-back-543-0)[\[543\]](#refnote-543) The Tax Cuts and Jobs Act set the individual mandate penalty at $0 starting in 2019.[&#8202;](#ref-back-hatchsays-1)[\[57\]](#refnote-hatchsays) The CBO estimated that the change would cause 13 million fewer people to have health insurance in 2027.[&#8202;](#ref-back-544-0)[\[544\]](#refnote-544)

The 2017 Individual Market Stabilization Bill was proposed to fund cost-sharing reductions,[&#8202;](#ref-back-545-0)[\[545\]](#refnote-545) provide more flexibility for state waivers, allow a new "Copper Plan" offering only catastrophic coverage, allow interstate insurance compacts, and redirect consumer fees to states for outreach. The bill failed.

By 2019, 35 states and the District of Columbia had either expanded coverage via traditional Medicaid or via an alternative program.[&#8202;](#ref-back-546-0)[\[546\]](#refnote-546)

## In popular culture

_[SNL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SNL "SNL")_ presented a sketch in October 2009 about the legislation's gridlock, with [Dwayne Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dwayne_Johnson "Dwayne Johnson") playing an angry President Obama confronting three senators opposing the plan.[&#8202;](#ref-back-547-0)[\[547\]](#refnote-547)

The show aired another sketch in September 2013 with [Jay Pharoah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jay_Pharoah "Jay Pharoah") as President Obama rolling out the plan to the public, and [Aaron Paul](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aaron_Paul "Aaron Paul") and other cast members playing ordinary Americans helping him in advocating for the legislation.[&#8202;](#ref-back-548-0)[\[548\]](#refnote-548)
